<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to Isaac Targier to the Preface, where the authors explain the moral and educational value of the illustrations contained in the volume.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'H' with a floral and foliate woodcut border.</image-desc>How virtuous examples can awaken the soul to goodness is a lesson taught to us not only by sacred and secular history <note>original: "heilige en onheilige geschiedenissen." In this context, "unholy" or "secular" simply refers to world history or non-biblical accounts.</note>, but also by daily experience.

And if there is anything that provides further occasion for this, it is the contemplation of paintings and printed illustrations; regarding the latter, as they serve our current purpose, we shall briefly say a few words.

First, one cannot deny that the portraits of great men—whose virtue and wisdom shone like bright, twinkling stars during their lives for the benefit of both State and Church—arouse in noble souls a certain desire for high esteem, and even for imitation. There seems to be an innate impulse within them that moves them, through observing the faces of these heroes, to trace their entire way of life in historical records <note>The original term <term>gedenk schriften</term> refers to memoirs or chronicles used to preserve the memory of notable figures.</note> and to adopt their virtues as their own. The same may rightly be said of depictions of edifying histories, which can leave no less of an impression on the heart, especially when they are wrought by the hand of an outstanding artist.

Secondly, one cannot be ignorant of how much light these images have provided to lovers of antiquity, especially when they are designed according to the thoughts of learned men or explained by a practiced pen, whether in prose or in verse.

Conversely, lewd prints can serve no purpose other than the corruption of all good morals:

<summary>This preface introduces the moral philosophy of the work, arguing that visual depictions of virtuous men and historical events serve to inspire noble character and educate the public.</summary>
<keywords>preface, moral education, art history, engravings, portraits, virtue, secular history, Dutch art theory</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, shifting from a warning against the corrupting influence of "lewd prints" mentioned at the end of the previous page to a celebration of the artistic and moral genius of Jan Luiken.</meta>

# PREFACE.

[...ing]: but let us not stir up that muddy pool: but rather move on to a fragrant garden <note>original: "Lufthof," literally a pleasure garden or "air-garden."</note> full of sweet-smelling flowers.

Who is unaware of the impression that the edifying printed illustrations <note>original: "Prentverbeeldingen"</note> by the famous Master of the etching art <term>etching art</term><note>Etskonst: the technique of using acid to bite designs into a metal plate for printing.</note>, JAN LUIKEN, have made on many? What art connoisseur does not stand enraptured when contemplating his works with attention and judgment, in which, through small figures drawn with a light and steady hand, a thousand wonders present themselves to the eye? Everything required for art flows into them: such as meaningful invention <note>original: "Vinding," referring to the artist's creative conceptualization.</note>, clever grouping <note>original: "Koppeling," or composition</note>, and the graceful proportions of the figures, natural movements, powerful passions, authentic clothing and customs of nations, excellent buildings, animals, and delightful landscapes, as well as a beautiful balance of light and shadow, without forced artificiality.

Let no one think that we bestow this praise upon him from ourselves alone; not at all: we speak from the mouths of artists and lovers of art <note>The term <term>Liefhebbers</term> refers to connoisseurs or collectors of the period.</note> who have long since granted him greater fame than our humble pen could ever add; and the manifold works of art of this ever-industrious man, which are preserved like jewels in the libraries and art chambers <term>cabinets</term><note>Kabinetten: specialized rooms or furniture used by collectors to display art and curiosities.</note> of the wise, will sufficiently immortalize themselves.

These *Scenes of the Early Christians* <note>original: "Tafereelen der Eerste Christenen"</note>, which we offer you, Reader, are, in the judgment of art connoisseurs, to be counted among his most excellent works; and were therefore judged doubly worthy of the printing press: the more so because the plates have suffered little or nothing, as is evident; since the work is generally much clearer and more vibrant than

<meta>catchword: te</meta>

<summary>This page praises the Dutch artist Jan Luiken, highlighting his technical skill in etching and his ability to convey deep emotion and historical accuracy. The authors frame his work as a virtuous alternative to corrupting secular imagery.</summary>

# P R E F A C E.

...previously came to light, which art connoisseurs <note>original: "Konstverstandigen"</note>, should they care to take the trouble, can compare against G. ARNOLD'S *True Portrait of the First Christians* <note>Referring to Gottfried Arnold’s *Wahre Abbildung der Ersten Christen* (1696), a highly influential work on the life of early believers.</note>.

The spiritual vitality of these scenes <note>original: "Tafereelen"</note> inspired the moral poet, CLAAS BRUIN, to adorn this work by placing a six-line caption beneath every print; while the poet PIETER LANGENDYK <note>A famous Dutch playwright and poet of the era.</note>, having already begun to set it into more extensive verse, was encouraged to complete his task more quickly, which has now been done; all the scenes facing the prints have flowed from his pen.

In the expectation that this artistic work will please, we wish the reader all happiness and prosperity.

->E. and J. VISSCHER.<-

<summary>This concluding section of the preface credits the poets Claas Bruin and Pieter Langendyk for the verse accompanying the prints and references Gottfried Arnold’s historical work on early Christianity.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the publishers' preface. The catchword "Eer-" from the previous page correctly leads into "Eerste Tafereel" (First Scene), marking the beginning of the poetic commentary by Pieter Langendyk that accompanies the illustrations of early Christian life.</meta>

SCENES OF THE...
1.

## First Scene.

Happy are those who worldly vanity
->With true earnestness despise,<-
From whom, however much she flatters our heart with a semblance of delight,
->No true pleasure is to be expected.<-
Though a King displays his splendor on an illustrious throne,
->Honored by his subjects,<-
He is himself a subject to the anxieties, which the crown
->Often make him hate;<-
Indeed, even if one possessed all earthly joy as his own,
->What could it truly give us?<-
A single moment robs us of that imagined treasure
->At the end of our life.<-
A Christian strives for joy that never fades,
->And God through His grace<-
Offers him, when His light shines in the dark heart;
->He, before it is too late,<-
Renounces worldly joy for that of Paradise,
->And dares in fearful times,<-
However much he is persecuted, to the praise of the great God,
->To confess the name of Prince Jesus;<-
Upon this confession, Christ’s Bride <note>A traditional theological term for the Church.</note> receives him,
->As one of her members;<-
Then the church pours out a vessel of blessing-oil <note>original: "zegenöly"; referring to the spiritual anointing and grace bestowed upon a new believer.</note>,
->In hymns of praise and prayers.<-

<note>The vocabulary below highlights key terms used in the Dutch text regarding spiritual devotion and the rejection of worldly status.</note>
**Vocabulary:** Scenes/Tableaux, vanity, delight/pleasure, King, Christian, Paradise, Jesus, Christ’s Bride (the Church), blessing-oil, hymn of praise.

<summary>This page contains the first poem of the collection, contrasting the hollow, anxiety-ridden life of worldly royalty with the eternal peace found by a Christian who renounces vanity to join the Church.</summary>
<keywords>vanity, kingship, Christian devotion, conversion, Christ’s Bride, spiritual joy, persecution</keywords>

# EARLY CHRISTIANS. I.
A
<meta>catchword: Two-</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a group of early Christians in a vaulted interior. In the foreground, a bearded teacher or elder in classical robes gestures toward a younger man who stands with a hand on his chest, looking humble and attentive. To the right, another man is seen kneeling in prayer with his face in his hands. In the background, other members of the community are seated on benches or standing, some engaged in conversation and others observing the interaction. The scene is framed by stone arches.</image-desc>

->*Examination of the Catechumens.*<-
<note>original: "Geloofsleerlingen"; literally "faith-learners," these were individuals preparing for baptism and full membership in the early Christian community.</note>

The Teacher’s zeal here strikes the student’s sorrowful heart,
And tests it through his sharp yet necessary questioning,
To see if it truly feels the pain <note>Referring to the "godly sorrow" or repentance for one's previous life and sins.</note> confessed to him
After diligent examination; whether in their days of life
Piety <term>godsvrucht: a deep, active reverence for God and the practice of religious duties</term> weighed the heaviest. This he considers most of all.
Happy would our century be, were such things practiced more.

**Vocabulary:**
Early Christians, Examination, Catechumens, Teacher, Piety

<summary>This page illustrates the rigorous spiritual examination of new converts in the early church, emphasizing the importance of sincere repentance and piety. The accompanying poem reflects a yearning for the strict moral and spiritual discipline of the early Christians to be restored in the author's own time.</summary>
<keywords>catechumens, religious examination, piety, early church, spiritual discipline, repentance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of scenes describing the initiation rites of the early church. Following the examination of the catechumens (converts) described on the previous page, this section focuses on the act of baptism itself, symbolizing the washing away of sins and spiritual rebirth.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
II.

## Second Scene.

He who, convicted of sins,
->Bows with humility before the Lord,<-
To be delivered from the old man <note>original: "ouden mensch"; a biblical term (Romans 6:6) referring to the unregenerate human nature before conversion.</note> and his desires,
As one of Christ’s precious members,
->Receives upon his confession,<-
->As proof that he is born again,<-
Baptism, the Red Sea <note>The author uses the "Red Sea" as a metaphor for baptism; just as the Israelites escaped Egypt through the sea while their pursuers drowned, the believer escapes their past while their "host of sins" is washed away.</note>, in which the host of sins
Drowns, that which was bound to the soul’s destruction;
->Baptism, a glistening garment,<-
->A bath in which the soul bathes,<-
A death to sin, through which man is born again,
A seal of faith, an anointing from God’s horn <note>original: "Godts hooren"; likely referring to the horn of anointing oil used for kings and priests in the Old Testament, symbolizing divine election.</note>.
->What a joy it would be for his congregation,<-
->If someone stepped upon the path of virtue!<-
Each thanked God and cried: blessed of the Lord,
Receive through the Savior’s favor your soul’s deepest desire,
->Drink with us the pure milk<-
->Of the Gospel <note>original: "Euangeliums"</note>, which for everyone<-
Provides refreshment in adversity and suffering,
Eat heavenly Manna <note>Manna: the bread from heaven provided to the Israelites in the desert, here symbolizing spiritual nourishment through the Eucharist or Word of God.</note> that strengthens you in trials and struggles;
->Descend into this clear flood of water,<-
->In which you must purify the body.<-
Just as the soul is cleansed of spiritual defects through spirit and fire, this baptism serves as a sign of that cleansing.

**Vocabulary:**
Second Scene, baptism, born again, sins, Gospel, Manna, confession, anointing

<summary>This page describes the ritual and spiritual significance of baptism in the early Christian tradition, portraying it as a "Red Sea" that drowns sin and a bath of rebirth. The text emphasizes the joy of the community in welcoming a new member into the faith.</summary>
<keywords>baptism, rebirth, confession, Red Sea metaphor, spiritual cleansing, Gospel, Manna, early church rituals</keywords>

EARLY CHRISTIANS. II.
2

->Administration of Baptism in a river.<-

After the trial of true faith and virtue has been passed,
The Confessor <note>original: "Belyder"; here referring to the believer who is publicly professing their faith through the rite of baptism.</note> is here immersed in the currents,
As a sign that sin has now been removed from him,
Through Jesus’ cross-blood <note>original: "kruisbloed"; a compound word referring to the blood shed by Christ on the cross, believed by Christians to wash away sins.</note>, and to hate the world’s wanton <note>original: "dert'le"; meaning frivolous, reckless, or worldly pleasures that lead one away from a spiritual life.</note> joy,
And to remain faithful to his Lord from this day forward.
Alas! would that the latter could be said of every candidate for baptism! <note>The author expresses a pious concern that not all who are baptized maintain their commitment to a virtuous life after the ceremony.</note>

<meta>This page continues the series of "Scenes" or "Tableaux" related to early Christian life. While the previous page (Scene II) focused on the physical and spiritual ritual of baptism by immersion, this page (Scene III) shifts to the internal and communal practice of prayer, using the Apostle Paul as a historical model.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
III.

### *Third Scene.*

A Christian soul flies from the earthly,
->On wings of prayer,<-
The heart lifts itself heavenward,
->Snow-white and unblemished;<-
God’s love purifies her from sins and flaws,
Thus she can speak mouth to mouth <note>original: "mond aan mond"; a phrase suggesting intimate, direct communication with the Divine without need for intermediaries.</note> with her Savior:

But he who lifts his hands upward,
->And mumbles with his mouth<-
Something that the heart does not perceive,
->Prays loosely and without foundation.<-
God knows what we lack; let each burn incense <note>original: "reukwerk"; a common biblical metaphor where the smoke of incense represents the prayers of the faithful rising to heaven.</note> to Him,
He demands obedience, not an empty sacrifice.

God’s chosen emissary,
->His Paul, goes before us,<-
He kneels at Tyre on the shore, <note>A reference to the biblical account in Acts 21:5, where the Apostle Paul and the local disciples knelt on the beach to pray together before he departed by ship.</note>
->The Savior gives ear:<-
For he was ignited by the fire of heaven’s spirit,
To break the stony hearts of the Heathens <term>Heathens: From the Dutch "Heidenen," referring here to the Gentiles or non-Jewish nations to whom Paul was sent to preach.</term>.

God listens through His mercy
->To a Christian voice,<-
That sacrifice reaches the Majesty <note>A title for God the King.</note>
->In the new Jerusalem,<-
And when many are gathered there to pray,
He descends with His power and glory into their midst.

<vocab>Christian, prayer, Savior, Paul, Tyre, Heathens, Jerusalem, mercy, glory</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the nature of true prayer, contrasting sincere spiritual devotion with empty, rote recitation. It cites the Apostle Paul’s prayer on the shore of Tyre as an example of the power of faith to reach God and transform the hearts of others.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, sincerity, Apostle Paul, Tyre, devotion, Holy Spirit, communal worship, mercy</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. III.

A 3
<meta>catchword: Four-</meta>

*An engraving depicting a scene from the Acts of the Apostles. In the center, the Apostle Paul stands in prayer, his hands clasped and his gaze turned toward the sky. He is surrounded by a group of men, women, and children who are kneeling or lying prostrate on a sandy beach. In the background to the left, a large sailing vessel is anchored. ->Paul’s prayer on the shore outside Tyre. Acts 21:5.<-

What soul is not here awakened to its chief duty,
Who sees Paul, enraptured in the spirit, uplifted?
The dunes are moved by the power of his words, <note>original: "redenkracht"; refers to Paul's persuasive eloquence and the spiritual weight of his speech.</note>
While the Tyrian shore provides a temple choir. <note>The poet suggests that the natural setting of the beach becomes a sacred space, rivaling a cathedral, through the act of communal prayer.</note>
One may lock the body in a narrow dungeon,
But the prayer of the devout cannot be constrained by any force.

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the biblical narrative of the Apostle Paul (Scene III) to the lives of early Christian martyrs during the Roman persecutions (Scene IV). It focuses on Saint Castulus, a high-ranking official in the palace of Emperor Diocletian who secretly aided the persecuted church.</meta>

->SCENES OF THE<-
IV.

## *Fourth Scene.*

How do you dare in the Imperial palace
To protect the sorrowful Christians,
O Castulus! <note>Castulus (died c. 286 AD) was a military officer and chamberlain to Emperor Diocletian. He secretly converted to Christianity and used his position in the palace to shelter other believers.</note> and time after time <note>original: "reis op reis"; literally "journey upon journey," meaning repeatedly or continually.</note>
Have mercy upon their calamity?
You shelter them at the risk of your life,
Facing exile and penance, as a Martyr.

Do you not fear the hatred of the tyrant? <note>Referring to Emperor Diocletian, notorious for the "Great Persecution" of Christians in the early 4th century.</note>
Do you dare, with these innocent sheep—
Pursued, chased away, spied upon everywhere—
To sleep in the midst of the wolves?
Do you alone strive for praise through virtue,
In the midst of the murderous court?

Yes, Castulus, illustrious hero!
You have undertaken something greater
Than if you had struck an enemy in the field,
And triumphed within Rome;
You triumph in this vault <note>original: "gewelf"; likely referring to the hidden chambers or catacombs where Christians met in secret.</note>
Through your virtues over yourself.

God's Angels keep watch here,
While you sit down together in unity,
Beseeching the true God day and night
For help, with a pure conscience.
He who is protected by heaven,
Fears neither Tyrants, fire, nor sword.

<term>Castulus</term>, <term>Christians</term>, <term>Martyr</term>, <term>Rome</term>, <term>God's Angels</term>, <term>Tyrants</term>

<summary>This page presents a poetic tribute to Saint Castulus, an official in the Roman imperial court who risked his life to shelter persecuted Christians. It contrasts military glory with the moral courage required to practice faith in a hostile environment.</summary>
<keywords>Castulus, Diocletian, martyrdom, early Christian persecution, Roman palace, secret worship, virtue</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS.
## IV.

->*Religious worship in the chamber of Castulus in the Emperor’s Palace.*<-

The pious Castulus fears no bodily danger
From the Emperor's hatred, now that he has given his dwelling
To religious worship <note>original: "Godsdienstoeff’ning"</note> for the Christian flock,
Who by tyranny are innocently driven out.
One sees here the devotion and faith in every face.
The soul <note>original: "’t Gemoed"</note> illuminated by God never shrinks from pain and scorn.

<meta>Five-</meta> <note>Catchword for the following page, "Five."</note>

<term>Castulus</term> (a high-ranking Roman official and martyr who sheltered Christians), <term>Religious worship</term> (the practice of communal prayer and liturgy), <term>Christian flock</term> (a metaphor for the early community of believers), <term>Emperor’s Palace</term> (the residence of Diocletian, where secret meetings occurred), <term>Tyranny</term> (referring to the Roman state's persecution), <term>Pious</term> (devout or godly)

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Saint Castulus, expanding on the previous scene's depiction of secret worship. SCENES OF THE
IV.

### *Fourth Scene.*

How do you dare in the Imperial palace
To protect the sorrowful Christians,
O Castulus! <note>original: "Kaſtulo"</note> and time after time
Have mercy upon their calamity?
You hide them at the risk of your own life,
Facing banishment and punishment, as a Martyr. <note>original: "Martelaar"</note>

Do you not fear the tyrant’s <note>original: "dwinglands," referring to the Emperor Diocletian</note> hatred?
Do you dare, with these innocent sheep—
Persecuted, hunted, and spied upon everywhere—
To sleep in the midst of the wolves?
Do you alone strive for praise through virtue,
In the midst of the murderous court?

Yes, Castulus, illustrious hero!
You have undertaken something greater
Than if you had struck down an enemy in the field,
And triumphed within Rome; <note>original: "Romen"</note>
You triumph in this vault <note>refers to the hidden chambers or cellars within the palace where the Christians met</note>
Through your virtues over yourself.

God’s Angels <note>original: "Godts Eng'len"</note> keep the watch here,
While you sit together in unity,
Pleading with the true God by day and night
For help, with a pure conscience.
He who is protected by heaven
Fears neither Tyrants, <note>original: "Tyrannen"</note> fire, nor sword.

<term>Castulus</term> (an officer of the palace who sheltered Christians and was eventually martyred by being buried alive), <term>Martyr</term> (one who bears witness to their faith through suffering or death), <term>Tyrants</term> (a reference to the Roman authorities, specifically Diocletian, who viewed Christianity as a threat to the state), <term>Rome</term> (the capital of the Empire and the site of the secret church), <term>Angels</term> (divine messengers believed to protect the faithful during their illegal gatherings).

<summary>This page is a poetic tribute to Saint Castulus, praising his courage for transforming the Emperor's palace into a sanctuary for persecuted Christians. It frames his secret hospitality not just as a physical risk, but as a spiritual triumph over the "wolves" of the Roman court.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Castulus, Roman Empire, Diocletian, persecution, secret worship, Christian martyrs, spiritual courage</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. IV.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a group of early Christians gathered for religious worship within a grand classical hall featuring massive columns and arched entryways. In the center, a man stands with arms outstretched, leading the assembly. Others are seated on the floor or kneeling in prayer; some appear to be reading or listening intently. The setting is identified by the caption as the quarters of Castulo in the Emperor's Palace.</image-desc>

4

->*Religious worship in the chambers of Castulus in the Emperor's Palace.*<-

The pious Castulus <note>original: "Caſtulo"; a Roman military officer and chamberlain to Emperor Diocletian who secretly converted to Christianity</note> fears no danger to his life
From the Emperor's hatred, now that he has given his home
For religious worship <note>original: "Godsdienſtoeff'ning"</note> to the Christian flock, <note>original: "Chriſtelyke ſchaar'"</note>
Who are innocently driven away by tyranny. <note>original: "tiranny"</note>
Here one sees the devotion and the faith in every face.
The soul illuminated by God never recoils from pain or scorn.

<meta>catchword: Five-</meta>

<term>Castulus</term> (a high-ranking Roman officer who utilized his position in the palace of Diocletian to hide Christians and hold services; he was eventually discovered and martyred), <term>Emperor</term> (refers to Diocletian, whose reign was marked by the "Great Persecution" of Christians), <term>Religious worship</term> (refers to the clandestine liturgical gatherings held in private homes or cellars to avoid arrest).

<summary>This page illustrates and describes a secret Christian assembly held in the Roman Emperor's own palace, facilitated by the officer Castulus. It emphasizes the contrast between the "tyranny" of the state and the peaceful, illuminated faith of the persecuted believers.</summary>

<keywords>Castulus, Saint Castulus, Roman Empire, Diocletian, Christian persecution, secret worship, martyrdom, religious devotion</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered sequence of "Scenes of the First Christians." While the previous page focused on the clandestine services held by Castulus in the Roman palace, this Fifth Scene shifts focus to a specific, dramatic instance of martyrdom: a mother witnessing her son’s execution.</meta>

SCENES OF THE

### Fifth Scene.

The Christian Mother <note>This represents a recurring motif in martyr literature where a mother encourages her child to face death for their faith, often inspired by the biblical account of the Mother and her seven sons in 2 Maccabees.</note> sees her son
Courageously climb upon a hill,
There tyrants <note>original: "dwingelanden"; refers to oppressive rulers or despots</note> snarl at him,
And torment him with much scorn and mockery,
She sees him kneel before the executioner’s sword,
And sings: Rejoice, God-dedicated souls,
My child receives the Martyr's crown! <note>original: "Martelkroon"; a common symbol in early Christian art representing the reward for those who died for their faith.</note>

Triumph! There fell the young man's head;
I have caught it in my lap.
Triumph! Triumph! Sing songs of blessing!
Now my child is not stolen from me,
Now the world shall not lead him astray,
Nor the arch-persecutor oppress him,
God, the Most High, be praised!

Tyrants, do you now stand amazed,
And filled with wonder;
Because I rejoice before your eyes?
Look at the head that you have struck down,
How it would rejoice, if it could speak,
And plead for your precious souls,
Like a noble Christian hero!

Come, cruel Executioners, <note>original: "Beulen"</note> seize me;
So that I too may die full of joy,
And earn heaven’s Martyr’s crown,
Where just now the Youth has stood.
I will willingly kneel down,
And pray to God for your souls;
Thus my Savior <note>original: "Heiland"</note> has gone before.

5.

<vocab>Christian Mother, Martyr’s crown, Youth, Savior, Executioners, Tyrants, oppressors</vocab>

<summary>This page depicts the martyrdom of a young man from the perspective of his mother, who celebrates his death as a spiritual victory rather than a loss. She catches his head in her lap and invites the executioners to kill her as well, mimicking the sacrifice of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Martyrdom, Christian Mother, Martyr's Crown, execution, sacrifice, religious courage, persecution</keywords>

FIRST CHRISTIANS. V.

B

<meta>catchword: Six-</meta>

A detailed copperplate engraving depicting a scene of Christian martyrdom. In the center foreground, an executioner has just beheaded a young man; the headless body remains kneeling while the executioner holds his sword. A woman, identified as the mother, kneels before the body, catching the severed head in a cloth held in her lap. To the left, a Roman soldier on horseback and other guards look on. In the background, there is a large circular stone tower and a city skyline featuring a domed building resembling the Pantheon. *The mother, catching the head of her child in her lap, sings a song of praise.*

See here a Mother, who cannot restrain her joy,
O wonder! now that she receives the head of her beheaded son
As it falls into her lap: indeed, she is moved to sing,
While her most precious pledge <note>original: "waardste pand"; a poetic term for a child, viewed as a precious gift or "pledge" entrusted by God to a parent.</note> takes possession of the crown
Of Heaven, which the Prince of Life <note>original: "Vorst des leevens"; a traditional title for Jesus Christ, specifically referencing him as the conqueror of death.</note> shall give to him.
Faith overcomes nature. <note>This phrase reflects the Stoic-Christian ideal that religious conviction can override natural human grief or the instinct for self-preservation.</note> Who can still tremble before the cross?

<vocab>First Christians, martyrdom, beheaded son, song of praise, faith, heavenly crown</vocab>

It emphasizes the theological theme that spiritual triumph and the promise of a "heavenly crown" can overcome even the most intense natural grief.</summary>

SCENES OF THE
VI.

## Sixth Scene.

O Agnes, immaculate and God-betrothed virgin!
How you have pleased your Bridegroom <note>The "Bridegroom" is a traditional mystical title for Jesus Christ, particularly in the context of martyred virgins who "married" Christ through their faith.</note>, who loves you,
When you saw yourself attacked on all sides
By the lustful,
And through steadfastness kept your honor unstained,
When you were dragged to the adulterous bed! <note>According to tradition, Agnes was sent to a brothel as punishment for refusing to marry the son of a Roman official, but she was miraculously protected from being touched.</note>
How the people heard you singing,
When one of the young men,
To violate your honor, seized you before everyone's eyes,
Upon which a fierce vulture <note>original: "felle gier"; likely a poetic metaphor for divine retribution or an angel striking the attacker. In standard legends, the attacker was struck blind or killed by a demon.</note> flew in his face!
You did not fear the wolf-teeth <note>A metaphor for the cruel, predatory nature of the persecutors.</note>
Of wicked tyrants;
But sang, and praised God, the highest Majesty,
As the Wedding was prepared for you in Paradise.
How proudly and courageously you spoke!
"The tyrant who so bloodily,
"So terribly stands before me, pleases my soul much more
"Than a young man, who strives for worldly honor,
"Who shows off with many colors,
"And smells of balsamic scents;
"I love the executioner much more, and meet him in spirit;
"Let him freely dye his broadsword in pure maiden's blood!
"That is the way to tread
"The clouds today!
"My soul leaps with joy out of the tortured body,
"Purified of everything earthly, as Jesus' dear Bride,
"In order to, in the Garden of gardens <note>original: "Hof der hoven"; a reference to the celestial Garden of Eden or Heaven.</note>,
"Praise her Bridegroom."

<vocab>Agnes, martyr, Bridegroom, Paradise, maiden’s blood, steadfastness</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts the martyrdom of Saint Agnes, celebrating her miraculous preservation of her virginity in a brothel and her joyful acceptance of execution as a means to join her spiritual bridegroom, Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Agnes, martyrdom, virginity, divine protection, bride of Christ, Roman persecution</keywords>

->SCENES OF THE<-

### Seventh Scene.

> The cemetery served as a sanctuary
> For Christ’s suffering community;
> They offered their sighs over the martyrs' bones,
> And strengthened themselves in their faith.
>
> Neither tombs, nor cellars, nor dark caves
> Can extinguish the fire of the heavenly spirit,
> In those who revere their Creator,
> Who can set their souls aflame.
>
> God is not bound by the splendor of any temple;
> A Christian must at all times
> And in all places profess His name,
> And praise Him by day and by night.
>
> He is present in the midst of His members,
> Even though they are gathered in a graveyard;
> The heart is His temple here on earth,
> Wherein He is truly worshipped.
>
> O precious, redeemed time!
> For those Christians who, every day,
> Stood as targets for the cruelest torments,
> How you brought joy to their hearts!
>
> When everyone faced torture and flaying <note>original: "pynigen en villen" refers to the graphic methods of execution used against early Christians</note>
> As the targets of a cruel executioner,
> And sought their help and refuge in God,
> Where were the denominational disputes <term>kerkverschillen</term> <note>Literally "church differences." The author is making a point that during times of intense persecution, the petty theological arguments that later divided the church did not exist.</note> to be found then?

<summary>This page reflects on the early Christians who were forced to worship in cemeteries and catacombs to escape persecution, emphasizing that true faith resides in the heart rather than in grand buildings. # THE FIRST CHRISTIANS. VII.

*An engraving depicting a group of early Christians gathered for a religious meeting in a cemetery. In the background, a large, fortified circular stone tower and high walls dominate the landscape, suggesting an ancient city like Rome. Several pyramid-shaped tombs and other stone monuments are scattered throughout the burial ground. The people are shown in various states of devotion: some are sitting on the ground, others are kneeling or prostrating themselves in prayer. In the foreground, a large sarcophagus is visible on the left, and a man sits in a prayerful pose on the right.*

->*Religious Meeting at a Cemetery.*<-

> The word of life <note>original: "woord des levens" — a common biblical reference to the Gospel or the teachings of Jesus.</note> is preached among the dead;
> The grave serves as a choir <note>In traditional church architecture, the "choir" is the area where the clergy and choir sit, often the most sacred part of the building. Here, the author suggests the cemetery itself has become a holy sanctuary.</note>. Thus tyranny rages
> Against early Christianity: yet neither hate nor oppression
> Restrains the holy heart from listening more to God’s commandments
> Than to the Imperial decree <note>Referring to the Roman laws that forbid Christian worship.</note>.
> Virtue fears no disaster; it looks toward eternal well-being.

<summary>This page uses a poem and illustration to depict the bravery of early Christians who gathered in cemeteries to preach the Gospel, choosing to obey God's laws over the restrictive decrees of the Roman Empire. It emphasizes that true faith and virtue look beyond earthly suffering toward eternal life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the series of "Scenes" (Tafereelen), specifically focusing on the Seventh Scene. SCENES OF THE

## *Seventh Scene.*

The cemetery served as a sanctuary <note>original: "kerk" — literally "church," but here referring to the place of assembly for the congregation.</note>
For Christ’s suffering congregation;
It sighs <note>In the sense of deep, prayerful yearning or lamentation.</note> over the martyr’s bones,
And strengthens itself in faith.
Burial places, vaults, nor caverns
Extinguish the fire of heaven’s spirit,
In him who fears his Creator,
Who can ignite his soul.
God binds Himself to no temple grandeur;
A Christian must at all times
And in every place confess His name,
And praise Him by day and night.
He is in the midst of His members <note>A biblical metaphor for the believers who make up the "Body of Christ."</note>,
Though they are gathered in the graveyard;
The heart is His temple here on earth,
In which He is truly worshipped.
O precious, ransomed time!
For Christians, who every day,
Stand as targets for the cruelest plagues <note>Referring to the various forms of Roman persecution and torture.</note>,
How you have gladdened their hearts!
When everyone, for torture and skinning,
Stood as the target of a cruel executioner,
And sought in God his help and refuge,
Where then were the church divisions <note>original: "kerkverschillen" — referring to theological disputes or denominational bickering which the author suggests vanish during times of shared suffering.</note> to be found?

VII.

<vocab>cemetery, Christ, martyrs, Creator, temple grandeur, Christian, church divisions</vocab>

<summary>This page provides a poetic reflection on the early Christians' practice of worshipping in cemeteries, emphasizing that true faith resides in the heart rather than in grand buildings. It concludes by noting that the intense persecution of the era served to unify the believers, leaving no room for theological disputes.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, secret worship, religious unity, persecution, temple of the heart, early church history</keywords>

# EARLY CHRISTIANS.
## VII.

<sig>B 3</sig>

->*Religious gathering in a Cemetery.*<-

The word of life is preached among the dead;
The grave serves as a choir <note>original: "koor" — while literally the area of a church for the choir or clergy, here it signifies that the cemetery has been transformed into a sacred sanctuary.</note>. Thus tyranny rages
Against early Christianity: yet neither hate nor tyranny
Restrains the holy heart from heeding God’s commands
More than the Imperial decree <note>The various legal edicts issued by Roman Emperors, such as Decius or Diocletian, which outlawed Christian assembly.</note>.
Virtue fears no disaster; she looks toward eternal Well-being <note>original: "eeuwig Wel" — referring to the state of eternal salvation or the "commonwealth" of heaven.</note>.

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of "Scenes" depicting the life and struggles of early Christians. While the previous page (VII) focused on worship among the dead in cemeteries, this page (VIII) shifts to "field preaching" or clandestine gatherings in the wilderness to escape urban persecution.</meta>

SCENES OF THE

### ->*Eighth Scene.*<-

Behold the Christians, gathered in the open field;
While tyranny bans them from every city;
The preacher <note>original: "leeraar" — literally "teacher," but here referring to the minister or pastor leading the clandestine service.</note> feeds their souls with edifying discourse,
And comforts them with the word that heralds their salvation.

Though an earthly power sets itself against Heaven’s Prince <note>Referring to Jesus Christ.</note>,
It shall be trodden down like a powerless worm;
Thus a tyrant rages in vain against the members of Jesus <note>original: "Jezus leden" — a theological reference to the Christian community as the "Body of Christ" (1 Corinthians 12:27).</note>,
Who in His own time shall repay him for those horrors.

The godly seed <note>A biblical metaphor (from the Parable of the Sower) representing both the Word of God and the growing community of believers.</note> grows, in meadows and valleys,
To spread the fruits of the kingdom of heaven;
Though the court <note>original: "het hof" — referring to the Roman Imperial court and the legal authorities enforcing the ban on Christianity.</note> roots this out, it grows sevenfold upon the land.

Thus you, Christians, could gather in the open field,
And grew in number like ears of corn,
Protected by God’s angels and His strong hand.

<sig>VIII.</sig>

<term>Christians</term> (followers of Jesus), <term>tyranny</term> (the oppressive rule of Roman emperors), <term>Godly seed</term> (the faithful or the gospel), <term>Scene</term> (a visual depiction or "tableaux"), <term>Heaven’s Prince</term> (Christ).

<summary>This page describes early Christians forced to worship in open fields and meadows after being banished from cities. It emphasizes that persecution only caused the faith to grow more rapidly, comparing the believers to a "godly seed" that flourishes despite efforts to uproot it.</summary>

<keywords>field preaching, clandestine worship, Roman persecution, growth of the church, agricultural metaphors, divine protection, religious endurance</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. VIII.

<meta>Artist: Jan Luyken</meta>

<image-desc>An etching by Jan Luyken depicting a religious gathering in an open field. A man in flowing robes stands on the left, gesturing as he addresses a large, attentive group of people—men, women, and children—seated on the ground. The background features a palm tree, a distant town with towers and domes, and a range of craggy mountains under a clear sky.</image-desc>

->Religious Gathering in the field.<-

***

Paganism <note>original: "Heidendom" — referring to the official Roman state religion and its polytheistic worship.</note> may boast of its marble temples;
But God’s chosen people, driven out by violence,
Are well content if only in the open field
They may exalt His name, through praise and thanks and prayer;
Yes, even though they wander through dismal forests by night.
The service of the Lord is bound to neither place nor time. <note>A reference to the theological shift from localized temple worship to the idea that God can be worshipped anywhere "in spirit and in truth" (John 4:24).</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the resilience of early Christians who, despite being excluded from cities and temples, found spiritual fulfillment in clandestine outdoor worship. It emphasizes the belief that the presence of the divine is not restricted to formal architecture but exists wherever the faithful gather.</summary>

<header>SCENES OF THE</header>

### Ninth Scene.

O **W**oman, who wishes to bear the name of Christian woman,
->But lives in idle pomp and splendor,<-
->Behold this precious hall,<-
Can it please your fastidious eye and sensuality? <note>The narrator addresses the wealthy women of his own time, challenging them to compare their luxury with the simplicity of the early church.</note>

You see no artistic tapestries in this room,
->The ceiling boasts no glittering gold,<-
->Nor East-Indian or Arabian wood.<- <note>A reference to the exotic, expensive timbers like ebony or sandalwood that were status symbols in wealthy 17th-century households.</note>
Here you learn how you must spend your precious time.

Once more, behold this hall, Ladies and Ma'ams,
->Here is no idle pride<-
->In needlework, lace, or silk;<-
Instead of fine cabinets, here stand simple <note>original: "slegte" — in this context meaning plain, humble, or ordinary, rather than poor quality.</note> looms.

Idleness is driven out of this beautiful room.
->This one sings while she spins<-
->Of how she loves her Savior,<-
Another winds a thread, the third is seen weaving.

O beautiful humility, how you can adorn a woman!
->O Christian daughters, how joyfully<-
->Do you handle your weaving,<-
And think, while you work, of neither splendor nor idle vanity!

A worldling <note>A person devoted to the interests and pleasures of this world rather than spiritual matters.</note> may lose themselves in earthly pomp:
->Pride brings the soul's destruction,<-
->Humility a glorious inheritance.<-
Happy are they who choose the better part. <note>An allusion to the story of Mary and Martha in Luke 10:42, where "choosing the better part" refers to prioritizing spiritual devotion over worldly distractions.</note>

<vocab>Ninth Scene, Christian woman, idle splendor, pride, looms, idleness, Savior, humility, weaving, soul's destruction</vocab>

<summary>This scene contrasts the vanity and luxury of worldly women with the industriousness and humility of early Christian women, who spent their time weaving and singing hymns. It emphasizes that true spiritual beauty comes from labor and devotion rather than expensive possessions.</summary>
<keywords>Christian womanhood, humility, industry, weaving, vanity, early Christians, domestic virtue, Jan Luyken</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. IX.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a large interior communal workspace. ->*Christian women living by themselves and supporting themselves <note>original: "zich geneerende" — in older Dutch, this meant to support oneself through a trade or craft, rather than the modern meaning of being embarrassed.</note> through spinning and weaving. 9*<-

> Useful occupation, joined with the duty of piety,
> Unites here a company <note>original: "rei" — literally a row or a choir, suggesting a rhythmic, harmonious group.</note> of Christian women,
> To which idleness and stubborn mistrust must yield.

> And think, with a heart that shuns strife and unrest:

--- Page 34 ---

SCENES OF THE
X.

### Tenth Scene.

When Emperor Decius <note>Decius (r. 249–251 AD) was the first Roman emperor to initiate an empire-wide persecution of Christians.</note> made innocent blood flow,
Like an arch-tyrant, and Prince Valerian <note>Valerian (r. 253–260 AD) continued the persecutions, famously executing Saint Cyprian and Saint Lawrence.</note>
Dared to strike God's congregation with his fierce claws,
Paul escaped their madness in a miraculous way.

You had to fear the wicked betrayal of your brother-in-law,
God-fearing Paul, O most noble Theban! <note>Paul of Thebes is traditionally regarded as the first Christian hermit. He fled to the Egyptian desert to escape both Roman persecution and a legal dispute with his brother-in-law over an inheritance.</note>
In the woods, you covered your body out of necessity with palm leaves,
Your estate was a cave, in place of the pleasure-house <note>original: "lusthuis" — a villa or summer manor typical of wealthy citizens.</note> taken from you.

Your drink was the sap of the tree, the palm fruit your food,
Thus, in solitude, the forest served as a Paradise to you,
There you praised your God with songs and Psalms.

Here you lived in peace, God-fearing hermit,
Until your soul was taken on high, after one hundred and thirteen years,
To be clothed by the angels with Zion's palms of peace.

<note>Emperor Decius, Prince Valerian, Paul, Theban, hermit, Paradise, Zion's palms of peace</note>

<summary>This page describes the life of Saint Paul of Thebes, the first Christian hermit, who fled Roman persecution and family betrayal to live a life of ascetic prayer in the desert. It portrays his transition from a wealthy nobleman to a simple hermit sustained by nature and faith.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Paul of Thebes, Emperor Decius, Emperor Valerian, asceticism, hermits, religious persecution, Egypt, palm trees, Zion</keywords>

FIRST CHRISTIANS.
10 IL

### X.

<image-desc>An engraving of Paul the Hermit. He is an elderly man with a long white beard and hair, clothed in a garment made of woven palm leaves. He stands in a prayerful posture with hands clasped, positioned between a large palm tree on the left and a rocky cave entrance on the right. The background features a winding river in a valley and distant mountains.</image-desc>

->Paul the hermit, clothed in palm-tree leaves.<-

And lives, clothed in leaves, in dark caves,
Where he implores his Creator, and laments the world's fall.

Because he extinguishes the light <note>A reference to the biblical command in Matthew 5:14-16 to not hide one's light under a bushel. The poet suggests that by living alone, the hermit is not letting his faith serve as an example to others.</note> that must shine for everyone:
Yet the pure intention makes this error good before God.

<page-num>IX</page-num>
<header>SCENES OF THE</header>

### Twelfth Scene.

Hear Alexander, in a purer garment,
Preaching the word of life for the edification of the people,
To kindle in everyone a fire for brotherly love and humility,
While he scorns the vanity that captivates the heart.

Now his spirit shines upon you, like the dawn,
And causes the mists of your prejudice to break;
Now from his mouth flow the sweetest streams of honey,
With which you, O Christians, satisfy your hungry hearts.

In this way, the simple are often chosen;
So that intellectual cleverness <term>spitsvinnigheid: refers to overly subtle, hair-splitting reasoning or pedantry</term> does not smother the truth,
Or stir up ecclesiastical strife out of pride.

O beautiful simplicity! Where have you been driven?
How seldom is a place given to you in the human heart!
Every vice is now varnished with the appearance of virtue.

SCENES OF THE

## *Thirteenth Scene.*

See how the Christians sit here in silence,
Listening attentively to the life-giving word,
Which, like a sharp sword, pierces the hard heart,
Or burns like a fire, to purify the conscience.

For one, the Scripture serves as comfort in adversity;
For another, it strengthens virtue through pure teaching;
It awakens another to penance and the conversion of the soul,
Or supports the wavering mind with strength.

Thus it serves as milk for this one, and solid food <note>original: "vaste spyze"</note> for that one,
Strengthening them from time to time in the pure faith,
Through which the Christian soul notices its transgressions,
And finds cause to praise God for His grace.

Give thanks, Christian, to your Lord for this precious treasure of salvation,
Far more powerful in brilliance than Aaron’s breastplate <note>A reference to the High Priest Aaron in the Old Testament, whose breastplate was set with twelve precious stones representing the tribes of Israel.</note>,
The pure Lights and Perfections <term>Urim and Thummim: Hebrew terms (אוּרִים וְתֻמִּים) for elements of the High Priest's breastplate used to determine God's will. The author here equates the Gospel to a superior, clearer form of divine revelation.</term> of the congregation,
The sun, before which the stars of the old law faded.

### *Thirteenth Scene.*

See how the Christians seated here in silence,
Listen attentively to the life-giving word <note>original: "levendmaakend woord"</note>,
Which like a cutting sword pierces the hard heart,

That one it strengthens in virtue, through unadulterated teaching;
Another it awakens to penance and soul-conversion,
Or supports with power the wavering mind.

Thus it serves this one as milk, and that one as solid food <term>Milk and solid food: a biblical metaphor from 1 Corinthians 3:2 and Hebrews 5:12, where "milk" represents basic teachings for new believers and "solid food" represents deeper spiritual truths for the mature</term>,
Which strengthens them from time to time in the pure faith,
Whereby the Christian soul notices its transgression,
And finds cause to praise God for His grace.

Far more powerful in brilliance than Aaron’s breastplate stones,
The pure <term>Urim and the Thummim: sacred objects or stones worn by the Jewish High Priest, used to determine God's will; here, the author suggests the Gospel is the true source of divine light</term> of the congregation,
The sun, before which the stars of the old law faded.

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XIV.

14
D 2
<meta>catchword: Five-</meta>

->**A Preaching or explanation of the Scripture by evening.**<-

No fear of death’s danger, nor the dark evening hour,
Could check the God-inclined soul in its holy zeal,
To reveal Jesus’ doctrine of love and wise decrees
To the multitude, touched by the Savior’s love.
Now (O morals! O deplorable times! <note>original: "ô zeden! ô beklaagelyke tyden!" This is a Dutch translation of the famous Latin lament by Cicero, "O tempora, o mores!", used here to criticize the lack of religious devotion in the author's own era.</note>)
A slight hindrance, or a mere passing shower, makes us shun the church.

<term>Prediking</term> (Preaching): The act of delivering a religious discourse or sermon.
<term>Schrift</term> (Scripture): The holy writings of the Bible.
<term>Jezus</term> (Jesus): The central figure of the Christian faith.
<term>Heiland</term> (Savior): A title for Jesus, emphasizing his role in the salvation of humanity.
<term>Zeden</term> (Morals/Customs): The social and ethical habits of a particular time or people.
<term>Kerk</term> (Church): Both the community of believers and the physical building for worship.
<term>Christenen</term> (Christians): Followers of the teachings of Jesus Christ.

<meta>This page transitions from the fourteenth scene to the sixteenth in Jan Luyken’s "Scenes of the First Christians." While the previous page praised the humble, secret gatherings of the early faithful, this scene introduces a sharp contrast: the rise of ecclesiastical pride and the corruption of the ministry through the historical figure of Paul of Samosata.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
XVI.

### Sixteenth Scene.

See here a Paul <note>This refers to Paul of Samosata, the 3rd-century Bishop of Antioch who became a symbol of arrogance and heresy in early church history.</note> depicted on this high throne,
Who with his pride <term>hovaerdy: an old Dutch term for vanity or haughty pride</term> annoys the simple folk;
He shouts, he stamps, he rants, and flails his hands,
Till his seat thrums, and all the temple walls
Echo with his sound and terrible clamor. <note>original: "vreesfelyk misbaar." This implies a loud, disruptive, and theatrical noise rather than a holy one.</note>
The Christian people easily perceive his arrogance;
Because, following in the footsteps of the princes of this earth,
He wasted a treasure of great value on this church-throne,
As a sign that he sought supreme rule,
And bound the other teachers to his ecclesiastical authority.
But his conceited language sounds disgusting in the ears
Of the truly pious, who listen for heaven's voice of salvation;
They look with loathing upon this Samosatene, <note>A follower or inhabitant of Samosata, specifically referring here to the followers of Paul’s worldly style of leadership.</note>
Whose words reach the ear, but do not enter the heart.
The time of apostasy <note>The "falling away" or abandonment of original Christian simplicity.</note> seems to approach in this manner:
Erudition undermines the simplicity of the fathers.
The teacher preaches much to find favor with the powerful;
This man cares nothing for preaching, but much for the *art* of preaching. <note>original: "predikkunst." The author distinguishes between the sincere act of sharing the Gospel and the theatrical, hollow performance of a professional orator.</note>

<vocab>Paul of Samosata, pride, Samosatene, art of preaching, church authority, the pious, clamor</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques Paul of Samosata as a symbol of the early church's decline into worldly vanity and theatrical oratory. It warns that intellectualism and a desire for power undermine the simple, heartfelt faith of the first Christians.</summary>
<keywords>Paul of Samosata, ecclesiastical pride, apostasy, corruption of the ministry, religious theater, early church history</keywords>

## THE FIRST CHRISTIANS. XVI.

->Paul of Samosata preaching in a conceited manner.<-

Unworthy namesake of the chosen Vessel, <note>original: "uitverkooren Vat." This is a biblical reference to the Apostle Paul (Acts 9:15). The author is contrasting the humble, holy Apostle with this "unworthy" namesake, the arrogant Bishop of Antioch.</note>
How does this delusion leave you so senseless and puffed up?
You extinguish the light of truth by blustering and raging.
Samosatene, <note>Referring to Paul of Samosata, who lived in the 3rd century.</note> place that invaluable treasure
In better hands, to be put out to interest <note>original: "op woeker uit te zetten." The poet uses a financial metaphor—putting money to work for interest—to suggest that the Gospel "treasure" should be managed by humble teachers who will make it grow in the hearts of the people.</note>:
God demands a gentle sound, not the noise of war trumpets.

<term>Paul of Samosata</term> — The Bishop of Antioch (260–268 AD) who became infamous for his theatrical preaching, his use of a high throne in the church, and his rejection of the divinity of Christ.
<term>Chosen Vessel</term> — A traditional title for the Apostle Paul, signifying he was an instrument chosen by God.
<term>Light of truth</term> — The pure spiritual message of the early church, which the author argues is obscured by loud, performative preaching.
<term>War trumpets</term> — A metaphor for a harsh, aggressive, or self-important style of speech that lacks Christian gentleness.

<summary>This page contains a moralizing poem criticizing Paul of Samosata for his arrogant and theatrical preaching style, contrasting him with the humility of the Apostle Paul. It serves as a warning against the corruption of the ministry through pride and worldly performance.</summary>
<keywords>Paul of Samosata, vanity, arrogance, preaching, Apostle Paul, chosen vessel, church history, 3rd century, theatricality, corruption</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic critique of Paul of Samosata, the 3rd-century Bishop of Antioch. While the previous page focused on his general arrogance and departure from the style of the Apostle Paul, this scene specifically attacks his theatricality and the way he encouraged his congregation to applaud him like an actor in a play.</meta>

SCENES OF THE...

## Seventeenth Scene.

Conceited teacher, blind of heart,
Who exalts yourself because your words
Charmed the vain and simple folk,
Whether they be subtle or confused;
The sound merely bounces off the temple walls,
Met by the empty clapping of hands.

Schismatic, <note>original: "Scheurmaaker." A term for someone who causes a "schism" or a formal split within the Christian Church, usually through false teaching or rebellion.</note> who twists the scriptures,
Why do you lay snares for Jesus’ Church of the Cross? <note>original: "kruiskerk." This likely refers to the "Church under the Cross," a term used for the true, humble, and often persecuted church, as opposed to the worldly, wealthy institution Paul of Samosata built.</note>
How you argue and dispute every day,
So long as you are not the one "crowing at the top." <note>original: "Zo lang uw haan niet boven kraaijt." A Dutch idiom meaning to be the master or to have the final, most dominant word.</note>
Does the clapping of hands charm you so?
Then go and let yourself be heard in a theater. <note>original: "Schouwburg." A public theater or playhouse. In the early church, theatrical performances were often viewed as immoral, making this a biting insult for a bishop.</note>

Perverted preacher, who through the art
Of select, curious paradoxes,
But no true virtues, strives for favor,
And furrows your brow in a hundred wrinkles;
You have lost the sword of truth,
And engage in nothing but a shadow-fight. <note>original: "spiegelstrijd." Literally a "mirror-battle." It refers to a mock battle or fighting against an imaginary opponent—empty posturing rather than meaningful spiritual work.</note>

XVII.

<term>Schismatic</term> — A person who creates a division or "schism" within a religious body. Paul of Samosata was eventually deposed for his unorthodox views on the nature of Christ.
<term>Church of the Cross</term> — A concept emphasizing the humility and suffering of Christ and his followers, contrasting with the "theatrical" and "vain" church described in the poem.
<term>Theater (Schouwburg)</term> — Historically, the church fathers were highly critical of the theater. Comparing a pulpit to a stage was one of the gravest insults a preacher could receive.
<term>Shadow-fight</term> — A metaphor for intellectual vanity; arguing over trivial points or performing for an audience rather than fighting for genuine spiritual truth.

<summary>This page presents a poem condemning the vanity of Paul of Samosata, specifically mocking his desire for congregational applause and his theatrical preaching style. It warns that such performative ministry abandons the "sword of truth" for empty fame.</summary>
<keywords>Paul of Samosata, theatrical preaching, schism, vanity, Church of the Cross, church history, early Christian critiques</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XVII.

<image-desc>An etching by Jan Luyken depicting a preacher in a high, canopied pulpit addressing a large congregation inside a grand, vaulted hall. Many members of the audience are seated on benches or the floor, while in the foreground, several men stand with their arms raised as if in acclaim or prayer. Below the image is a centered caption in Dutch: "Handgeklap van 't Slechtervolk onder de predicacie." The artist's signature "I. Luiken" and the plate number "17" are visible in the bottom right corner of the etching's frame.</image-desc>

->Hand-clapping of the common people during the sermon.<- <note>original: "Slechtervolk." In 17th-century Dutch, this referred to the "simple" or "common" people, though here it carries a derogatory tone regarding their lack of discipline.</note>

Where are you, O Christians? Not before the theater-stages <note>original: "Schouwtoonneelen." The early Church Fathers strictly forbade attending the theater, which they associated with paganism and moral decay.</note>
Of blind Heathendom, but before the pulpit.
Here, no hand-clapping is fitting: that wanton commotion
Serves only to softly stroke the teacher’s heart with vanity. <note>original: "eerzucht." Literally "honor-thirst," referring to the pride or ambition of a preacher who seeks applause rather than converts.</note>
Therefore, if you hear something good, thank your Creator in the spirit,
And beseech Him that He may heal you of sinful evil.

<summary>This page illustrates and poetically critiques the practice of applauding during sermons, a behavior associated with the vanity of Paul of Samosata. It reminds the reader that the church is a place for spiritual healing and gratitude to God, not a theater for entertaining the masses.</summary>

<keywords>Paul of Samosata, theater vs church, vanity, clapping in church, Jan Luyken, 17th-century Dutch poetry, sermon etiquette</keywords>

TAREAUX OF THE
XVIII.

<image-desc>A faint, full-page engraving serves as the background for the text. It depicts an elderly, bearded man (Origen) seated and teaching from a large open book. He is surrounded by several attentive young men in a room with classical architectural elements.</image-desc>

### Eighteenth Tableau.

Behold the pious Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD) was one of the most prolific and influential early Christian theologians, known for his asceticism and his massive output of biblical commentary.</note>
In the midst of fellow believers,
Who has opened the Lord’s book,
And teaches, lesson by lesson,
How a Christian ought to behave,
And guard against the snares of the abyss. <note>original: "des afgronds laagen." A poetic reference to the temptations or traps set by hell.</note>

O gray and aged man,
How one sees you surrounded by youths,
Full of attention and faith,
Despite the heathens' cursed company! <note>original: "vloekgespan." This refers to the pagan Roman authorities and groups who opposed and eventually persecuted Origen.</note>
You rescue them from Satan’s snares,
And arrows aimed at the heart.

When the strength of your voice failed,
You wrote for edification, comfort, and instruction,
And brought many thereby to conversion.
God’s Spirit flows like a stream of honey,
First from your tongue, taming the passions,
Now you nourish the heart through wise writings. <note>Origen is said to have kept seven shorthand secretaries and seven copyists busy at once; his written works were essential to early Christian thought even after he could no longer speak in public.</note>

**Vocabulary:** Origen, fellow believers, Christian, heathen, conversion, wise writings.

<summary>This page celebrates the influential church father Origen, depicting him as a devoted teacher who continued to convert and edify the faithful through his writings even in his old age.</summary>
<keywords>Origen, early church fathers, Christian education, biblical scholarship, conversion, asceticism, religious writing</keywords>

# SCENES OF THE... <note>The full title is likely "Scenes of the First Christians" based on the series.</note>
## XIX.

<meta>The central text is contained within a rectangular plate-marked or shaded frame. Faint ghost-like imagery is visible behind the text, likely from an engraving on the reverse side of the leaf depicting a city in turmoil.</meta>

### *Nineteenth Scene.*

->See Constantinople in distress.<-
The earth shakes and trembles from below.
Palaces shake from the thunder,
And everything quivers before death.

This one prays, that one is seen running in fear.
The night is turned into day,
By heavenly fire and lightning strikes:
Everyone baptizes, or lets themselves be baptized out of fear. <note>In the early church, major disasters like earthquakes or fires often led to mass "emergency baptisms" by those who had previously delayed the sacrament.</note>

Learn here, you who despise God’s long-suffering <term>long-suffering: 'lankmoedigheid' in the original Dutch, referring to God's patience and restraint in the face of human sin</term>
Through luxury and carelessness,
How He warns you that your wickedness
Leads you to the pool of the abyss. <note>original: "poel des afgronds", a reference to the lake of fire or the depths of hell.</note>

His thunder sometimes stirs your heart.
In adversity you call upon Him:
But had you stayed on that path during prosperity,
His lightning would never have struck you with sorrow.

<vocab>Constantinople, Scene, God's long-suffering, lightning strike, pool of the abyss</vocab>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XIX.

<meta>Sig: E 3</meta>
<meta>catchword: Twin-</meta>

->*The amazement of the people in Constantinople who are being baptized in the streets.*<-

Here, terror shows its power upon the conscience through the storm,
As everyone seeks baptism now, fearing Heaven’s wrath. <note>During the 4th and 5th centuries, it was common for people to delay baptism until their deathbed or a moment of great crisis, believing the sacrament would wash away a lifetime of sin all at once.</note>

SCENES OF THE
XX.

### *Twentieth Scene.*

A teacher chosen for the task of baptism,
Baptizes young and old in this font.
Blessed is he who, being born again, <term>born again: ‘weêrgebooren,’ a central Christian concept referring to spiritual rebirth and the start of a new life in faith</term>
Understood the power of Christ’s baptism,
Through a moisture purer than the water brooks
That flow down from Mount Lebanon! <note>Mount Lebanon is frequently cited in the Bible as a symbol of purity, majesty, and life-giving water.</note>

His baptism consisted of fire and spirit,
First bestowed upon his Apostles,
On the most precious Feast of Pentecost, <note>Pentecost: A festival celebrating the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the followers of Jesus, described in the New Testament as appearing as "tongues of fire" and enabling them to speak in many languages.</note>
Divided into tongues, like heavenly sparks;
It was that baptism that broke the heart,
Through which men spoke in every language.

That fire pierced through to the heart and kidneys, <note>original: 'hart en nieren'. In historical and biblical language, the kidneys (or "reins") were believed to be the seat of a person’s deepest emotions and conscience, much like we speak of the "gut" or "heart" today.</note>
Each person was baptized in the name of the Lord,
And allowed themselves to be guided by His Spirit,
Which stirred them into a holy zeal.
Blessed are those who turn toward Him,
And thus learn the true power of baptism!

<summary>This page provides a spiritual commentary on the sacrament of baptism, contrasting physical water with the "fire and spirit" of the Pentecost. It emphasizes that true baptism is an internal transformation that guides the believer's conscience and zeal.</summary>
<keywords>Baptism, Pentecost, Holy Spirit, rebirth, Apostles, Lebanon, spiritual zeal</keywords>

SCENES OF THE

### Twenty-second Scene.

Here they hold the Lord's Supper <note>The *Avondmaal* (literally "Evening Meal") refers to the communion service or Eucharist, practiced here in a manner reflecting Jan Luyken's Anabaptist-leaning views on simplicity.</note> with simple hearts,
To remember the Lord’s death and suffering.
One does not see the wine poured from gold or silver vessels:
Here stands a basket full of bread: everything is humble <note>The original Dutch *slegt* translates here as "plain" or "unadorned," emphasizing a lack of worldly luxury rather than poor quality.</note> and good.

Each calls upon the Savior, who feeds their souls,
And wishes to give them drink from the pure fountain of His grace;
Thus the evil venom of the red dragon <note>A biblical reference to the "great red dragon" of Revelation 12, symbolizing Satan or the forces of persecution.</note> cannot harm them;
Thus they triumph over all adversity.

Although the bread is not broken here in a spacious temple,
Although this humble building has neither pillar nor marble threshold,
The believing heart is the house in which the Lord dwells:

Where such hearts are, Jesus is in the midst;
There He holds the Lord's Supper, there He listens to prayer,
Thus His Bride, the church, <note>In Christian theology, the "Bride of Christ" is a metaphor for the collective body of believers or the Church.</note> is crowned at His table.

<image-desc>An engraving by Jan Luyken (signed "I. Luiken" in the lower right) depicting a group of early Christians celebrating the Lord's Supper (Communion). ->*The conduct of the Lord's Supper in a plain and simple manner.* <note>The original Dutch *ſlechte* (modern: *slecht*) here carries the archaic meaning of "plain," "simple," or "unadorned," rather than "bad."</note><-

Must give way; that preview of the eternal Wedding Feast, <note>A biblical reference to the "Marriage Supper of the Lamb" (Revelation 19:9), which represents the final union of Christ and his followers.</note>
Is here most devoutly shared in spirit,
That its power may release the soul from guilt and punishment.
How many there are who join the Table of the Lord,
Yet leave the true wedding-garment <note>An allusion to the Parable of the Wedding Banquet (Matthew 22), where the "garment" symbolizes the inner spiritual preparation or righteousness of the believer.</note> of piety behind!

SCENES OF THE
XXIII.

### *Twenty-third Scene.*

The Christian who must constantly wander,
->Deprived of human help and comfort,<-
->Is comforted by God in his great distress;<-
Yes, even though he must lose his fatherland,
->And finds none of his kin,<-
He shall, in dismal darknesses,
Not miss that Savior <term>Savior: translated from the Dutch *heiland*, meaning "Healer" or "One who brings salvation."</term> of his soul,
->Who loves him for his faithfulness.<-
One must tread upon sharp thorns,
When traveling to blessed Eden. <note>A reference to the heavenly paradise as the final destination of the difficult journey of life.</note>

But what refreshment <term>refreshment: translated from *verquikking*, a term for spiritual or physical revival and cooling.</term> it must give,
->When many believers meet one another,<-
At the risk of their very lives,
In the forest where lions and bears live. <note>Likely a metaphorical reference to the dangers and "wild beasts" of persecution, or to the actual wilderness where Christians were forced to hide.</note>
->How they press each other’s hand!<-
Their love cannot be quenched
By any kisses of love or any embraces,
->Since everyone burns with zeal.<-
This fire of love cannot be extinguished,
Since it receives its nourishment from above.

SCENES OF THE
XXIV.

### *Twenty-fourth Scene.*

See how heaven’s elect <term>elect: translated from *uitverkoornen*, referring to those chosen by God for salvation.</term>
Greet each other with a kiss,
A kiss of peace, which those reborn,
For the love of their Savior, thus
Give to one another with pure hearts,
For the refreshment of the soul in this life.

The lowly <note>original: *de kleine*, literally "the small," referring to those of low social or spiritual status.</note> are not here by the great
Despised because of this love.
Envy remains excluded here.
Mistrust has no power over them.
Each speaks from the reflections of their heart.
One knows no Joab’s kiss <note>A reference to the biblical General Joab, who killed Amasa while pretending to greet him with a kiss (2 Samuel 20:9).</note> or pretense.

Learn here, you deceitful people,
How groundless your pretense is;
You appear to long for Canaan <note>original: *Canaän*. In this context, it represents the Promised Land or the state of heavenly grace.</note>,
Yet you lose your way on the path of life.
Do you imagine you can appease the wrath of God?
Oh no, He recognizes your Judas-kisses <note>A "Judas-kiss" is an act of betrayal disguised as a gesture of affection, referencing Judas Iscariot’s betrayal of Jesus.</note>.

<vocab>Scene, Savior, refreshment of the soul, envy, Joab, Canaan, Judas-kisses</vocab>

SCENES OF THE
XXV.

### *Twenty-fifth Scene.*

Eulampia saw her brother standing,
In the midst of the fierce murderers <note>original: *moordenaaren*. In this context, it refers to the Roman executioners or soldiers carrying out the death sentence.</note>,
She pushed through, and spoke: oh cruel barbarians,
I am comforted to go to death with him.
I shall not avoid your violence, oh cruel ones!
Do not tear me away from my dear brother,
Punish me with him: yet I do not count it as punishment
To suffer your rage for Jesus’ sake.
    There is no fate so glorious for my soul,
As that I may die for the true faith,
And thus attain the crown of glory <note>A common Christian symbol representing the reward in heaven for those who remained faithful unto death.</note>,
From my God, before whom I kneel down.
She then joyfully embraces her brother,
Who is boiled in oil beside her,
And though the executioner stokes the fire with blind zeal,
God remains their protector in all that heat.
    Though the body burns, the soul is not touched;
It flies on high, on wings of prayers,
And feels no pain from the body here below;
Because it glows in God’s perfect love.

<summary>This page illustrates the martyrdom of Saint Eulampia and her brother Eulampius, who were executed by being boiled in oil. The poem emphasizes Eulampia's voluntary sacrifice and the belief that spiritual devotion can transcend physical suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Eulampia, Saint Eulampius, martyrdom, boiling oil, religious persecution, crown of glory, faith, execution</keywords>

# SCENES OF THE
## XXVIII.

### *Twenty-eighth Scene.*

Martin sees in wintertime
A poor man shivering from the cold;
So he cuts his cloak in two,
(Though others mock him, we are told)
And clothes the poor and naked man,
So that the godly praise his name:
Since he gives all that he can,
And seeks by power to proclaim
How the pious never lack the will,
To help the one who's pleading still.

Antiquity <term>aêloudheid</term> <note>The author refers to ancient Christian tradition or hagiography, specifically the life of St. Martin written by Sulpicius Severus.</note> says that Christ appeared
In that half-cloak Martin had bestowed,
And thus His teaching was endeared,
Which to the people He had showed:
That he who clothes the poor in need,
Clothes Christ Himself in word and deed, <note>A reference to Matthew 25:40: "Truly I tell you, whatever you did for one of the least of these brothers and sisters of mine, you did for me."</note>
And shall his great reward then find
With the Most High, who is so kind,
Who turns all grief and woe to grace,
When hearing in that holy place
The joyful voice: "Come, enter in,
The new Jerusalem to win."

<note>original vocab: Martinus (Martin), Chriftus (Christ), Jerufalem, aêloudheid (Antiquity/Ancient tradition), godtvruchtigen (The Pious/Godly), wintertyd (Wintertime)</note>

<summary>This page recounts the famous legend of Saint Martin of Tours, who cut his cloak in half to clothe a freezing beggar, later discovering the beggar was Christ in disguise. It emphasizes the theological concept that acts of charity toward the poor are acts of service to God Himself.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Martin of Tours, charity, cloak, Christ, mercy, winter, New Jerusalem, piety</keywords>

# THE FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXVIII.

*Martin cuts his cloak in two, and gives half of it to a poor man. 28*

The strict Baptist <note>original: "Dooper" — referring to John the Baptist.</note> once spoke to the Jewish crowds:
"If you have two coats, give one away out of love." <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 3:11: "Anyone who has two shirts should share with the one who has none."</note>
The pious Martin, after a beneficial deliberation,
Wishes here to spare but half of his single coat <term>lyfrok</term>. <note>The "lyfrok" or tunic was Martin's only garment available to share while on duty as a Roman soldier. The poet highlights that while John the Baptist advised giving away an extra garment, Martin went further by dividing his only one.</note>
Illustrious example of a compassionate heart,
How few followers you have now in times of need and pain!

<note>original vocabulary: Eerste Christenen (First Christians), Martinus (Martin), Dooper (Baptist), Joodsche schaaren (Jewish crowds), lyfrok (tunic/coat), medelydend hert (compassionate heart)</note>

<summary>This page features a poetic reflection on the charity of Saint Martin of Tours, comparing his act of splitting his only cloak with John the Baptist’s biblical command to share from one's surplus. It serves as an exhortation to the reader to practice similar compassion in an age where such virtue is seen as rare.</summary>

XXIX.

### *Twenty-ninth Scene.*

Behold how here many pious ones <note>original: "vroomen" — refers to the devout early Christians known for their simple, godly lives.</note>
Out of love have come together,
And sit peacefully at the table <note>original: "disch" — an archaic term for a dining table or board.</note>.
Here one sees the poor beside the rich,
Each one lets their love show:
While everything is full of contentment.

Here is no respect of persons,
Nor great ones who mock the lowly,
Through pride, vanity, or arrogance <term>hovaerdy</term>. <note>The Dutch "hovaerdy" denotes a specific type of haughty pride or conceit that the author contrasts with Christian humility.</note>
The Spirit of the Lord hovers in the midst
Of the Love Feast <term>Liefdemaaltyd</term>, <note>Commonly known by the Greek term "Agape," these were communal meals eaten by early Christian communities to express their unity and provide for those in need.</note> where they pray
That their food <note>original: "spys" — an old word for food, provisions, or sustenance.</note> may be blessed.

The rich man, who has the food brought,
Willingly wishes to mingle among the poor:
He looks at the heart, not at the clothing.
Where has that love, alas! <note>original: "laas!" — a contraction of "helaas," meaning "alas" or "woe."</note> gone,
That gave such meals,
So sweet, sincere, and well-bestowed! <note>original: "wel besteed" — literally "well-spent," implying the resources were used for a noble and righteous purpose.</note>

<summary>This page describes a "Love Feast" (Agape), a communal meal where early Christians of all social classes gathered in equality. The poem serves as a moral critique of the author's own time, lamenting the loss of such sincere communal charity and lack of social hierarchy.</summary>
<keywords>Love Feast, Agape, equality, early Christians, charity, humility, communal meals</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXIX.

H
<meta>catchword: The-</meta>

*A copperplate engraving by Jan Luyken depicting a "Love Feast" (Agape) among early Christians.
