->A Love Feast.<-

Where did Brotherly Love ever give a fairer sign of victory <note>original: "zegeteeken" — a trophy or monument signifying a triumph.</note>
Of conquest over greed <term>geldzucht</term>, <note>Literally "money-lust," one of the primary vices the communal life of early Christians was intended to counteract.</note> envy, and hatred,
Over proud arrogance and many other failings of the soul,
Than there, where the heart always stands open along with the table,
In spite of the rage and cruelty of tyrants? <note>A reference to the Roman authorities who often persecuted Christians for their "secret" communal gatherings.</note>
But the banishing of discipline <term>tucht</term> has banished this custom. <note>The author suggests that the "Agape" fell out of use as the strict moral standards and communal accountability—the discipline—of the early church began to decline.</note>

SCENES OF THE
XXX.
XXX.

### *Thirtieth Scene.*

*Here appears the goodness of the wealthy,*
*Who do not falter in love:*
  *But share in this spacious hall*
  *With their Brothers, the poor members*
  *Of Christ’s church, here below,*
  *The remainder of the Love Feast <term>Liefdemaal</term>.* <note>original: "Liefdemaal" — refers to the Agape feast, a communal meal shared by early Christians to express their unity and provide for the needy.</note>

*Quite different it is in these days*
*Among those who bear the name of Christians,*
  *Yet are servants of their bellies;*
  *Who revel, carouse, and feast excessively <note>original: "slampempen" — an archaic Dutch term for wasteful, gluttonous partying.</note>,*
  *Who mock the poor in their need,*
  *And turn everything to misuse.*

*These shall hereafter lack everything,*
*And receive nothing from God the Lord;*
  *But see Lazarus <note>A reference to the biblical parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus (Luke 16), where a poor man is rewarded in heaven while a wealthy man who ignored him is punished.</note> in Abraham's bosom,*
  *Where the poor shall eat the manna,*
  *Seated at Jesus' table of love,*
  *With those who fed them in their time of need.*

<note>The vocabulary list from the original text: goodness, the wealthy, Love Feast, Christians, Lazarus, Abraham's [bosom], manna, Jesus, table of love.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the reflection on the Christian Love Feast by contrasting the selfless charity of the early church with the perceived decadence of contemporary society. It warns that those who neglect the poor will be excluded from the heavenly banquet.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "First Christians" series, specifically focusing on Scene XXX. While the previous page introduced the concept of the communal Love Feast, this page depicts the practical charitable outcome of that gathering: the distribution of surplus food to the destitute. It emphasizes the moral obligation of the wealthy to care for the poor, a central theme in Jan Luyken’s depictions of the early church.</meta>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXX.
30

*An engraving by Jan Luyken set in a large, beamed interior. On the left side, several people sit at a long table laden with food; a servant or host pours liquid from a large pitcher. In the center, a man in robes distributes food from a platter to a group of men, women, and children who approach him with outstretched hands. In the foreground, various large jugs, a basket, and a sack rest on the floor. A cupboard filled with bowls stands in the background. The engraving includes the artist's signature "J. LUIKEN" and the number "30" in the bottom right corner of the frame.*

->Distribution of the surplus food to the poor.<-

Here the surplus of food and drink is given
  To many needy souls, of all support deprived.
  O blessed is he, who when the pinch of need <note>original: "nypen van den nood" — an idiom describing the sharp, pressing pain of extreme poverty.</note> is felt,
Follows this example, and by a noble longing led,
  Pours out his wealth with joy into the poor man's home.
But alas! How many fall short in this solemn duty.

SCENES OF THE

### Thirtieth Scene.

->Here the goodness of the wealthy is revealed,<-
->Who do not falter in their love:<-
->But share, within this spacious hall,<-
->With their Brothers—the poor members<-
->Of Christ's church here below—<-
->The leftovers of the Love Feast. <note>original: "Liefdemaal" — referring to the Agape feast, a communal meal shared by early Christians to signify their fellowship.</note><-

->It is quite different in these days<-
->Among those who bear the name of Christians,<-
->Yet are servants of their own bellies;<-
->Who gorge, swill, and feast decadently, <note>original: "slampempen" — an archaic Dutch term for wasteful, riotous living or excessive feasting.</note><-
->Who mock the poor in their distress,<-
->And turn everything to misuse.<-

->These shall hereafter be deprived of all,<-
->And inherit nothing from God the Lord;<-
->But shall see Lazarus in Abraham’s bosom, <note>A reference to the biblical parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus (Luke 16), where the poor man finds comfort in heaven while the rich man suffers.</note><-
->Where the poor shall eat the manna,<-
->Seated at Jesus’s table of love,<-
->Together with those who fed them in their need.<-

<meta>This page continues the series "Scenes of the First Christians" by Jan Luyken. While the previous page focused on the general distribution of surplus food by the early church community, this scene highlights a specific historical figure, Empress Flaccilla, as a royal exemplar of those same charitable values.</meta>

SCENES OF THE

### *Thirty-first Scene.*

Flaccilla, Empress of the mighty Roman Empire,
Is not seen here within her precious halls
Among ladies-in-waiting, who boast of gold and diamonds;
But in a hospital, <note>original: "gasthuis" — In the 17th century, this referred to a charitable institution providing medical care and lodging for the poor and infirm.</note> a testament to her humility.

She refreshes the poor here, as if she were their equal.
She tastes the food of the sick, washes cups, dishes, and bowls,
Even hands them spoons, and brings for their comfort,
All that might serve the sick, so that they do not perish.

With good reason then, O jewel of Empresses!
Did Prince Theodosius <note>Theodosius I (the Great), who reigned from 379–395 AD. He was the last emperor to rule over both the Eastern and Western halves of the Roman Empire.</note> love you for your virtues,
He whom God blessed, for the joy of Christendom.

But the Most High Prince <note>Referring to God.</note> held you in even greater esteem;
The Emperor kissed you as the ruler of the earth:
But God brightened your crown with a fairer Majesty.

<sig>XXXI.</sig>

---

<term>Flaccilla</term> (Aelia Flaccilla) — The first wife of Roman Emperor Theodosius the Great. She was famous for her personal devotion to the poor and disabled, reportedly visiting hospitals to wash and feed the sick with her own hands.
<term>Roman Empire</term> (roomsche ryk) — The historical setting, emphasizing that even at the height of imperial power, Christian humility was practiced.
<term>Virtues</term> (deugden) — Specifically the "theological virtues" of faith, hope, and especially charity (caritas), which Luyken aims to promote.
<term>Majesty</term> (Majesteit) — Here used as a play on words, contrasting her earthly title as Empress with the spiritual "majesty" granted by God for her humble service.

<summary>This page depicts Empress Flaccilla performing humble tasks in a hospital, serving as a moral example of how even the highest nobility should practice Christian charity. It contrasts her earthly imperial status with the spiritual glory she earns through her service to the sick and poor.</summary>
<keywords>Empress Flaccilla, Theodosius the Great, Roman Empire, charity, humility, hospital, Christian virtues, Jan Luyken</keywords>

TABLEAUX OF THE
XXXI.

### *Thirty-first Tableau.*

Flacilla, Empress of the mighty Roman Empire, <note>Aelia Flaccilla (died 386 AD), the first wife of the Roman Emperor Theodosius I, renowned for her personal service to the disabled and the poor.</note>
Is not beheld here in her costly halls
Among ladies, who boast of gold and diamonds;
But in a hospital, <note>original: "gasthuis" — a charitable house for the sick and poor, often run by the church.</note> an evidence of her humility.

She refreshes the poor here, as if she were his equal.
She tastes the food of the sick, rinses cups, dishes, and bowls,
Even gives them spoons, and has brought for their relief,
Everything that serves the sick; so that he may not perish. <note>original: "bezwyk" — to succumb to illness, faint, or die.</note>

With reason must you then, O pride of Empresses!
Be loved by Prince Theodosius <note>Theodosius I (the Great), who reigned 379–395 AD and was a major proponent of Nicene Christianity.</note> for your virtues,
Whom God has blessed, to the joy of Christendom.

But the Most High Prince <note>Referring to God as the King of Kings.</note> held you in even greater esteem,
The Emperor kissed you indeed as the ruler of the earth:
But God shone upon your crown with a fairer Majesty.

<term>Flacilla</term> (Aelia Flaccilla) — A Roman Empress known for her piety; historical accounts claim she personally washed the wounds of the sick.
<term>Empress</term> (Keizerin) — The highest female rank in the Roman Empire.
<term>Roman Empire</term> (roomsche ryk) — The vast state centered on Rome (and later Constantinople) where Christianity became the state religion under Theodosius.
<term>Hospital</term> (gasthuis) — In the 17th-century Dutch context of this book, this refers to a charitable institution for the needy.
<term>Theodosius</term> (Theodozius) — The Roman Emperor who was the last to rule over both the Eastern and Western halves of the Empire.
<term>Christendom</term> (Christenheid) — The collective body of Christians or the Christian world.
<term>Majesty</term> (Majesteit) — Here used to compare the worldly glory of a queen with the divine glory of a saint.

<summary>This page describes the piety of Empress Flacilla, who abandoned the luxuries of the palace to personally serve the sick in a hospital. It portrays her charity as the source of her true "majesty" in the eyes of God.</summary>
<keywords>Empress Flacilla, Theodosius the Great, hospital, charity, humility, Roman Empire, early Church virtues</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXXI.

XXXI

*An engraving by Jan Luyken depicting Empress Flaccilla (Flacilla) performing acts of charity. In the background, a large, arched hall or courtyard contains numerous other beds where the sick are being tended to by various figures. ->The Empress Flaccilla serves the poor in the Hospital.<-

But his Flaccilla’s heart is overwhelmed with joy,
When she may tend to the sick in the hospital, <note>original: "gasthuis" — In this period, a "guest house" or hospital was a church-run charitable institution providing medical care and shelter for the destitute.</note>
And serve them in their need: oh, matchless virtue!
You proud misers, <note>original: "vrekken" — greedy people or skinflints.</note> who dare to boast of your thrift,
This Empress could easily condemn you at the Judgment. <note>Refers to the Last Judgment; the poet suggests that if an Empress can give so much, those who hoard wealth have no excuse.</note>

### *Thirty-first Scene.*

Flaccilla, Empress of the mighty Roman Empire,
Is not seen here within her precious halls
Among ladies-in-waiting, who boast of gold and diamonds;
But in a hospital, a testament to her humility.

She refreshes the poor here, as if she were their equal.
She tastes the food of the sick, washes cups, dishes, and bowls,
Even hands them spoons, and brings for their comfort,
All that might serve the sick, so that they do not perish.

With good reason then, O jewel of Empresses!
Did Prince Theodosius <note>Theodosius I (the Great), who reigned from 379–395 AD. He was the last emperor to rule over both the Eastern and Western halves of the Roman Empire.</note> love you for your virtues,
He whom God blessed, for the joy of Christendom.

But the Most High Prince <note>Referring to God.</note> held you in even greater esteem;
The Emperor kissed you as the ruler of the earth:
But God brightened your crown with a fairer Majesty.

<meta>This page continues the series "Scenes of the First Christians" by Jan Luyken. Following the previous entry regarding Empress Flaccilla's charitable works in the hospital, this page introduces Bishop Deogratias of Carthage, another historical figure celebrated for his extreme devotion to the sick and destitute.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
XXXII.

# Thirty-second Scene.

The Overseer Deogratias <note>original: "Deogratias" — a Latin name meaning "Thanks be to God." He was the Bishop of Carthage (c. 454 AD) known for his immense charity during the Vandal invasion.</note>
Uses the church for sick Christians,
Who thank him for his diligence:
Because he was always helpful to them
In having soft beds spread out,
And preparing what was refreshing. <note>original: "verquiklyk" — restorative food or drink given to the weak.</note>

Though he finds no reward here on earth,
He is willing to give his last penny
To the pious driven from their inheritance, <note>This refers to the Roman captives brought to Africa by Geiseric the Vandal; Deogratias sold church gold and silver to ransom them and provide for their needs.</note>
And knows that he thus visits God’s Son
In his sick Brother,
For whom he (after God) serves as protector.

Thus he finds rest in his soul,
He who will not enslave himself to earthly things,
Always ready with help and gifts of love,
Because God repays him sevenfold,
Through the soul’s contentment here below,
And hereafter the sweetness of blessed Eden.

<term>Overseer</term>: (Dutch: *Opziender*) A term used here for a Bishop or church leader, emphasizing their role as a guardian of the community.
<term>Deogratias</term>: A historical Bishop of Carthage who famously turned two large basilicas into hospitals to house captives.
<term>Sick Christians</term>: (Dutch: *Chriſten kranken*) The infirm and suffering members of the early church.
<term>Son of God</term>: A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 25, where service to the "least of these" is considered service to Christ.
<term>Eden</term>: The biblical paradise, used here to represent the eternal reward of Heaven.
<term>The Pious</term>: (Dutch: *vroomen*) The faithful or devout believers.

<summary>This page describes the charitable works of Bishop Deogratias of Carthage, who converted his churches into hospitals for the suffering and used his resources to care for the displaced.</summary>
<keywords>Deogratias, Carthage, charity, hospitals, bishop, early church, Vandals, refugees, mercy</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXXII.

->*The Bishop Deogratias uses the Church as a Hospital.*<-

O Deogratias, showered with gifts! <note>original: "met gaaven overſtort" — referring to the spiritual graces and material means he used for charity.</note>
How gloriously your name <note>The name Deogratias is Latin for "Thanks be to God."</note> is united with your duties,
Now, through your foresight to alleviate disasters,
The Church becomes a hospital, <note>original: "gaſthuis" — literally a "guest house," the historical term for a hospital or refuge for the needy.</note> and every sick person becomes a Teacher! <note>The poet suggests that the suffering of the poor teaches the faithful more about Christian duty than a sermon.</note>
Faithful Bishop, continue thus in works of love.
That ministry of preaching <note>original: "Predikdienſt" — the service of the word.</note> will strengthen the heart more than your voice.

<meta>catchword: Drieën-</meta>
<meta>artist-signature: I. Luike 32</meta>

SCENES OF THE <note>The full title of this 17th-century work is "Scenes of the First Christians."</note>
XXXIII.
<meta>Faint ghost text or catchword visible in the bottom-left corner.</meta>

### Thirty-third Scene.

Basil <note>Saint Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD), Bishop of Caesarea, who was famous for his care of the poor and the "Basiliad," a massive complex of hospitals and almshouses.</note> receives, in a time of great scarcity, <note>original: "dieren tyd" — refers to the famine that struck Caesarea in 368 AD.</note>
Many sorrowful Christians, wandering due to persecution,
Who leave their earthly Fatherland to gain the heavenly one,
And please their God through their diligence.

Though they have lost house and home, and dear kinsfolk,
They shall hereafter inherit the salvation of eternity,
And never be deprived of the glory of the heavenly rays,
By which they are enlightened, and shine far and wide.

The patriarch <term>oudvader: A title used here to denote a Church Father or venerable leader.</term> serves them himself at the table with his food,
So that he might teach, through his virtue and example,
How everyone ought to be of service to the stranger.

Thus he shall one day hear the voice of the Savior:
"Enter into the joy of the Lord, you were my chosen,"
"For I was a stranger, and you offered me shelter." <note>This is a direct reference to the words of Jesus in the Gospel of Matthew 25:35.</note>

**Vocabulary:** Basil, Christians, persecution, earthly Fatherland, eternity, patriarch, stranger, Savior, Shelter.

<summary>This page depicts Saint Basil the Great providing food and refuge to displaced Christians during a famine, emphasizing the importance of personal service and hospitality as a core Christian virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Basil the Great, Caesarea, famine, refugees, Christian charity, hospitality, Matthew 25, Jan Luyken</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the thirty-third tableau, which depicted Saint Basil the Great's charity. The previous page ended with a catchword "Four-" leading into "Thirty-fourth" (Vierendertigſte). This continues the series of moralizing scenes from the early history of the church, moving from the acts of the Church Fathers to the foundational example of Christ himself.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
XXXIV.

## *Thirty-fourth Tableau.*

The Lord girds his body with a linen cloth,
And washes the feet of the Apostles.
He teaches how they must serve each other as servants,
And use no mastery <note>In this context, "mastery" (meeſterſchap) refers to the exercise of worldly power or hierarchical dominance over others.</note>, to their own curse:
But Simon Peter dares to refuse this,
Whereupon the Savior says, "unless
I wash your feet, you have no part in me."
Learn here, you who dare to rise on the throne like Kings,
And are called the servant of servants, <note>This is a pointed critique of the Papacy; the Latin title *Servus servorum Dei* (Servant of the servants of God) was used by Popes, whom the poet suggests are actually seeking worldly "mastery."</note>
How God dooms your vain lust for power,
And condemns your mastery, you hypocrites of the earth,
With his own mouth, as something of no value.
Why then do you coerce a tender spirit,
Which your church-rule <term>kerkvoogdy: The governance, oversight, or administrative authority of the church hierarchy.</term> does not nourish?
If God is to wash you in the pure pools
Of his grace and goodness,
Then abandon your mastery, lay down the miter-crown: <note>The miter is the ceremonial headdress of bishops and the Pope, used here as a symbol of high-ranking ecclesiastical office.</note>
For humility receives immortality as its reward.

<vocab>Apostles, Simon Peter, Savior, church-rule, miter-crown, humility, immortality</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates and reflects upon the biblical scene of Jesus washing the feet of his disciples, using it as a sharp critique of ecclesiastical hierarchy and the "mastery" sought by church leaders. It argues that true spiritual reward comes from the humility of a servant rather than the power of a throne.</summary>

<keywords>Jesus Christ, Foot Washing, Humility, Anti-clericalism, Apostles, Simon Peter, Papal critique, Servant of Servants</keywords>

FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXXIV.

<image-desc>An engraving of a biblical scene where Jesus Christ is kneeling on a stone floor to wash the feet of his disciples. Other disciples are arranged around them, seated on benches or standing, watching the event with various expressions of contemplation. Lutken 34" is visible in the bottom right corner.</image-desc>

->Of all the lessons we find in the Holy Word<-
->On humility, that glorious example still shines out<-
->Of the Savior's meekness in the washing of the feet,<-
->Through which he halts all desire for pomp and pride.<-
->O Christians! Let anyone who wishes praise arrogance;<-
->Your sun must descend if it is to rise one day in Salem. <note>original: "Salem"; a poetic name for Jerusalem, here referring to the Heavenly Jerusalem or the Kingdom of God.</note><-

<term>First Christians</term>, <term>meekness</term>, <term>humility</term>, <term>Savior</term>, <term>Salem</term>, John 13, washing of the feet, Christians, Jesus, Disciples.

SCENES OF THE
I
XXXV.

## Thirty-fifth Tableau.

See here, most skillfully depicted,
How frugally the first Christians ate,
And sat as if in the presence of the Lord,
Who provides each with food and drink.

Here the herbs of the field are stewed,
Thus to satisfy their hunger,
Not to overload the body
With food that dulls the spirit. <note>The author suggests that heavy, indulgent food numbs the mind and soul, preventing spiritual clarity.</note>

Here no host pressures his guest
To drunkenness or revelry. <term>revelry; original: brafferyë, meaning gluttonous or rowdy feasting</term>
The wine here gives birth to no frenzy, <term>frenzy; original: raazernyë, referring to the madness or violent behavior caused by intoxication</term>
Nor does anyone suffer any distress.

In this way the body is best strengthened,
The spirit refreshed to praise God,
Who grants all blessings from above,
When the Creator's goodness is observed.

<vocab>Tableau, Christians, The Lord's, Creator, revelry, stewed, frenzy</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a poem describing the temperate dining habits of the early Christians, emphasizing that they ate for nourishment and spiritual clarity rather than gluttony or intoxication. It highlights the moral value of moderation as a way to honor the Creator's gifts.</summary>
<keywords>Christian temperance, moderation, communal dining, early Christians, gluttony, spiritual health, sobriety, gratitude</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XXXV.

<image-desc>An etching by Jan Luyken depicting the simplicity of the early Christians. In the center, a group of four people sits at a rough wooden table for a meal; two have their hands folded in prayer, while one man cuts a loaf of bread. To the left, a woman tends a large pot over an open fire, and another worker sits on the ground nearby. The scene is set in an open landscape with a well and figures working in the distance, a prominent palm tree, and a mountain in the background. A simple stone building stands on the right. The artist's signature "I. Luyken" is visible in the bottom right corner of the print.</image-desc>

->The simplicity of the first Christians, eating bread and salt along with roots and herbs 35<-

> You who dare to trample the laws of moderation
> Through wanton delicacies that mock simple necessity,
> See here the Christians, content with bread and salt,
> Thanking their Creator for that blessed enjoyment.
> How you shall—when the eternal feast they strive for is their reward—
> Yet long for a single drop, to cool your burning flame! <note>The poet refers to the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus (Luke 16:24), where a wealthy man who feasted sumptuously in life later begs for a drop of water to cool his tongue in the afterlife, while the poor man is comforted.</note>

SCENES OF THE
<sig>XXXVIII.</sig>

### *Thirty-eighth Scene.*

Yonder one sees Didymus kneeling before the bloody broadsword,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And the martyr’s fire ready where the body must burn,
After he had magnanimously protected Theodora, <note>Original: "Theodoor". In the 4th-century account, Didymus, a soldier, swapped clothes with the virgin Theodora to allow her to escape a brothel where she was being held; both were eventually executed.</note>
And snatched her from the power of unruly scoundrels.

She, seeing the wicked brood swarming around her savior,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Pushes through, and cries out: see here yet more Christian blood,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Slash my neck with one stroke, burn or blaze and rage,
So we may offer together two stainless souls.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She kneels as she sees his head fall into the sand,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Receives the same fate, and is burned with him,
While their souls praise God, their salvation and refuge.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Steadfast Martyr! <note>The original Dutch uses the feminine *Martlaares* here.</note> Faithful Martyr! <note>The original Dutch uses the masculine *Martelaar* here.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With what joy you embrace one another in the blessed kingdom,
Which infinitely surpasses all worldly joy!

<vocab>Didymus, Theodora, Martyr (male), Martyr (female), Scene, Christian blood, broadsword, martyr’s fire</vocab>

<summary>This poem depicts the joint martyrdom of Didymus and Theodora, highlighting their selfless sacrifice and the spiritual triumph of their execution over earthly suffering. It emphasizes the theme of "stainless souls" finding eternal joy in heaven after facing the "bloody broadsword" and fire.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, Saint Didymus, Saint Theodora, sacrifice, persecution, early church, heaven, execution</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<header>SCENES OF THE</header>

## Thirty-Ninth Scene.

See here the Christian people persecuted, everywhere cast out,
At Tyre <note>An ancient Phoenician port city in modern-day Lebanon, which was a major site of Christian persecution under the Roman Empire.</note> condemned to death for their faith,
They call upon Jesus in their great distress,
While the wild beasts' den is opened in the arena <note>original: "strydperk", literally 'battle-ground' or arena where gladiatorial games and executions took place</note>.

But the African lion, so sharp of maw and claw,
Nor the hungry bear spring upon this prey;
The hand of the Lord, which has checked their rage,
Wishes to enlarge His congregation through this miracle.

Stubborn pagan people, how you remain so blinded,
To shed that innocent <note>original: "onnozel", which in this period meant 'innocent' or 'harmless' rather than 'silly'</note> blood so mindlessly?
God warns you in time, so that everyone may notice.

Your cruelty is tolerated by Him for a time,
A Christian thereby wins the happiness he seeks,
And the blood of the Martyrs is the seed of the Christian Church <note>This is an English translation of the famous Latin phrase by Tertullian: "Semen est sanguis Christianorum." It suggests that the death of believers only serves to grow the faith.</note>.

<sig>XXXIX.</sig>

<vocab>Christian people, Tyre, African lion, Martyrs, Christian Church, miracle, pagan people</vocab>

<summary>This poem describes a miraculous event in the arena of Tyre where wild animals refused to harm condemned Christians, concluding with the famous sentiment that persecution only strengthens the Church.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, Tyre, divine intervention, lions, Roman persecution, Tertullian, early Church history</keywords>

SCENES OF THE <note>The original Dutch title "TAFEREELEN DER" is likely followed by a word like "Martelaren" (Martyrs) on the title page.</note>
XL.

### *Fortieth Scene.*

Lawrence the Martyr,
In Rome during times of distress,
Sentenced to suffer death,
After much persecution and danger,
Steadfastly endures all pains
Which seem unbearable to the flesh.

Thus he addresses his persecutor,
Who has him roasted on a gridiron <note>Saint Lawrence was one of the seven deacons of the city of Rome under Pope Sixtus II who were martyred in the year 258. Tradition states he was grilled alive.</note>:
"If you wish to satisfy yourself with my flesh,
Turn me over: this side is well-done."
The tyrant does as he desires,
Turning him onto his other side.

"Taste (he says further) my flesh is done;
Will it taste best raw or roasted?
I fly to God, oh vile barbarian!
Your fire cannot touch my soul!"
Thus courageously died in God’s grace
Lawrence, the Christian Scaevola <note>original: "Scebola". This refers to Gaius Mucius Scaevola, a legendary Roman hero who famously held his right hand in a fire without flinching to demonstrate his resolve. By calling Lawrence a "Christian Scaevola," the author highlights his extraordinary bravery under torture.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the martyrdom of Saint Lawrence in Rome, focusing on his famous defiance while being roasted on a gridiron. It compares his stoicism under torture to the ancient Roman hero Mucius Scaevola.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Lawrence, martyrdom, Rome, gridiron, Christian history, Mucius Scaevola, persecution, religious endurance</keywords>

# THE FIRST CHRISTIANS.
XL.
<meta>Artist signature: J. Luiken</meta>
<meta>Plate number: 40</meta>
<meta>Catchword: One-</meta>

<image-desc>A detailed historical engraving depicting the martyrdom of Saint Lawrence. In the center, Saint Lawrence is shown lying on a large metal gridiron (rooster) placed over a bed of burning coals and wood, with smoke and flames billowing upward. He is propped up on his right arm, pointing his left hand toward a Roman official or tyrant standing on an elevated dais in the upper right. This official is dressed in Roman military attire and is accompanied by several guards and attendants. A dense crowd of soldiers and onlookers surrounds the scene of execution. In the background, classical Roman architecture is visible, including a domed structure and a tall monumental column. In the foreground to the left, an executioner kneels to stoke the fire with a tool.</image-desc>

*Lawrence, roasted on a gridiron in Rome, undauntedly addresses the Tyrant.*

Who can grasp the power of God’s grace and aid,
Who sees Lawrence here upon the glowing iron,
While through patience he despises the pains that strike him,
And speaks with courage (O Rome! do you not hear?):
"Do you desire, O Judge, to satiate yourself with my flesh?
Then turn the body o’er; this side is roasted enough."

### *Fortieth Scene.*

Lawrence the Martyr,
In Rome during times of great distress,
Sentenced to suffer death
After much persecution and danger,
Steadfastly endures all the pains
Which to the flesh seem unbearable.

Thus he addresses his persecutor,
Who has him roasted on a gridiron:
“Do you wish to satiate yourself with my flesh?
Turn me over: this side is cooked through.”
The tyrant does as he desires,
Turning him onto his other side.

“Taste it,” (he continues) “my flesh is done.
Will it taste best raw or well-roasted?
I fly to God, O vile barbarian!
Your fire cannot touch my soul!”
Thus courageously died in God’s grace
Lawrence, the Christian Scaevola. <note>Gaius Mucius Scaevola was a legendary Roman hero who famously held his hand in a fire without flinching to show his resolve; the poet suggests Lawrence’s Christian faith makes him an even greater hero of endurance.</note>

### Forty-second Scene.

Bound at hands, neck, and feet,
->In a dreary, dark cave,<-
This Christian waits for his God,
Who can sweeten <note>original: "verzoeten" — a common 18th-century Dutch term for making hardships bearable through divine comfort</note> all his calamities;
Though the dungeon deprives him of light,
His soul retains a clear vision.

The cruel executioners, who plague him,
->Are in a worse darkness,<-
They miss the path of life,
While a thousand sins gnaw at them;
->They are in the enemy's <note>referring to the spiritual enemy, or Satan</note> slavery,<-
The Christian, though bound, remains free.

Though it stinks in this dreary dungeon,
->The scent of Christian virtue,<-
Which refreshes the heart and gladdens the soul,
Retains its strength and makes him stronger;
->He is an incense-offering;<- <note>The martyr’s suffering is compared to aromatic incense burned as a sacrifice to God</note>
That the Christian heart burns for Jesus.

SCENES OF THE

## *Forty-third Scene.*

What do you attempt through your floggings,
Persecutors! to compel the spirit
Of the Lord’s people? Every lash
Shall serve as a spur for them,
To rouse them to those virtues,
Which God teaches them from day to day.

Why do you wish to make them mourn through pain,
And tear the body open with iron claws; <note>original: "krauwels" — hooked instruments or metal rakes used to torture the flesh</note>
You do not tear them away from Christ,
Whose blessing shall one day make them rejoice,
And who, after this suffering, shall protect 
His lambs with His heavenly staff.

God shall heal those scourging-welts
In Salem’s <note>Referring to the New Jerusalem or the heavenly paradise</note> blessed bowers,
With heavenly balm: the precious blood
Shed for them on Golgotha,
While you are eternally cast out,
When you, O executioners! have spent your rage.

<page-num>XLIII.</page-num>

<vocab>floggings, Persecutors, spur, virtues, iron claws, Christ, lambs, heavenly staff, scourging-welts, Salem, bowers, heavenly balm, Golgotha, executioners, spent your rage</vocab>

<summary>This poem addresses the futility of physical torture, such as flogging and the use of iron claws, against the early Christians. It asserts that such suffering only strengthens their virtue and leads to eternal healing in heaven, while their executioners face eternal rejection.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, persecution, torture, spiritual resilience, New Jerusalem, Golgotha, floggings, 18th-century Dutch poetry</keywords>

# EARLY CHRISTIANS. XLIII.

43

->Flogging with twigs and rods; and tearing open the body with iron claws.<-

Horrific pain through cruel floggings,
Which here tragically befalls the innocent! <note>original: "onnoozelheid" — in this context, it refers to the moral purity and harmlessness of the martyrs rather than a lack of wisdom.</note>
Yonder the body is torn open with an iron claw. <note>original: "krauwel" — a many-pronged hook or rake used in Roman torture to strip flesh from the bone.</note>
Alas! who can yet restrain their heart from sighing,
Who beholds such a scene of horrors?
Oh, blessed is he who in that distress trusts in God!

SCENES OF THE

## Forty-fourth Scene.

Why do you incite, fierce tyrants,
The lions with their sharp claws and teeth
Against these defenseless people?
No leaden balls <note>original: "loode kogelen" — likely referring to the *plumbatae*, lead-weighted balls attached to Roman scourges, though the Dutch word "kogelen" would later come to mean bullets.</note> or hooks
Can make them forsake the true faith,
The rock upon which they stand safely.

Why do you scourge so fiercely with sinews <note>original: "peezen" — whips made from animal tendons or sinews, known for their toughness.</note>
The members of the Eternal Being, <note>A theological reference to the martyrs being part of the "Body of Christ."</note>
Who exhort you to virtue;
Who speak the word of their Lord,
And never seek revenge upon their persecutors;
But whose conversion would bring them joy?

Stop, and allow yourselves to be convinced,
Willingly bend your hardened hearts,
And take note of their steadfastness;
See how they abandon possessions and goods,
And hate not you, but your wickedness,
Which leads you into the devil’s snares.

FIRST CHRISTIANS. XLIV.

->*Cruel scourging with sinews, with leaden balls, and with barbed hooks, and the tearing apart by lions.*<-

---

->This scourging-fury exceeds all others by far,<-
->The leaden balls <note>original: "loode kogelen" — referring to the *plumbatae*, lead weights attached to the leather thongs of a Roman whip to increase the force of the blow.</note> and sinews <note>original: "peezen" — whips made from dried animal tendons, which were much tougher and more painful than standard leather.</note> strike the heart;<-

->Tear a martyr apart! Who can gaze upon this spectacle<-

### Forty-seventh Scene.

->Cruelty has reached its peak,<-

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. XLVII.

M 3

<meta>catchword: Eight-</meta>

->*Various cruel deaths and pains inflicted upon the Christians.*<-

What a heavy cross Christianity must endure here! <note>original: "kruis verdraagen" — to bear a cross, a metaphor for enduring suffering or trials in imitation of Christ.</note>
This one is crushed, another dragged, and that one—oh, horror!—is forcefully
Thrown from a great height, while others are put to death
By the striking of a stone; <note>original: "ſtooten met een ſteen" — this refers to stoning or being crushed by heavy weights, as seen in the foreground of the image.</note> over there, limbs are struck off;
Elsewhere the flesh is stretched and scorched. You, who live in freedom,
Fear God, and thank Him that He does not subject you to such a trial. <note>The author shifts from historical observation to a direct address, reminding the 17th-century reader that their religious liberty is a gift that many before them did not possess.</note>

<term>Christianity</term> (Chriſtendom): Here used to refer to the collective body of believers or the faith itself.
<term>Cross</term> (kruis): A common symbol for the suffering and martyrdom that a believer must "carry."
<term>Martyrdom</term> (martelaarschap): The act of suffering or dying for one's religious beliefs.
<term>Jan Luyken</term>: The famous Dutch poet and engraver (1649–1712) known for his vivid and often gruesome depictions of martyrs.
<term>Freedom</term> (vryheid): A reference to the relative religious safety of the Dutch Republic compared to the Roman persecutions.
<term>Persecution</term> (vervolging): Systematic hostility and ill-treatment, especially based on religious belief.

<summary>This page illustrates and describes a diverse array of execution methods used against early Christians, including being thrown from heights, dismembered, and crushed. The accompanying poem serves as an exhortation for the reader to be grateful for their current religious freedom.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, Jan Luyken, religious persecution, stoning, dismemberment, gratitude, early Christians, Roman Empire</keywords>

SCENES OF THE
L.
<meta>The Roman numeral XLIX is visible as faint bleed-through or ghost print above the heading.</meta>

### Fiftieth Scene.

Oh, all too inhuman atrocities!
   They bind the Christians upon wheels,
And turn them against spikes sharp of point, <note>original: "pennen" — these refer to sharp iron pegs or spikes fixed to torture devices like the breaking wheel or spiked rollers.</note>
Or let them roll down from the hills,
To perish upon the hard ground;
   Because their downfall is the intended goal.

Oh, great long-suffering of the pious,
Who do not shrink before even death itself,
   How marvelous are your strengths!
Though they are scorched or pierced through,
Their faithfulness has been proven to their God;
   They are forever out of danger. <note>The poet suggests that once the martyr dies, they are "out of danger" because they have reached the safety of heaven, beyond the reach of earthly persecutors.</note>

Though the Sledgehammer <note>original: "Moker" — a heavy mallet used to break the bones of the condemned, a common part of the execution by "breaking on the wheel."</note> bruises chest and limbs,
They have finished their struggle,
   Kept the Faith, and the crown
Assigned to them, received on high,
Where no shackles can oppress them,
   Beside the holy Lamb, and His throne.

<term>Long-suffering</term> (lydzaamheid): A central virtue in martyr literature, referring to the patient endurance of pain without renouncing one's faith.
<term>Breaking Wheel</term> (raden): An ancient and medieval torture device where the victim's limbs were broken with an iron bar or hammer while lashed to a large wagon wheel.
<term>The Crown</term> (de kroon): The "Crown of Life," a spiritual reward promised in the New Testament to those who endure trials.
<term>The Lamb</term> (het lam): A symbolic representation of Jesus Christ, often depicted in the Book of Revelation as being seated upon a throne.

<summary>This page describes the brutal execution of Christians using breaking wheels, spiked rollers, and sledgehammers. It emphasizes that despite these "inhuman atrocities," the martyrs find eternal safety and reward in heaven.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, breaking wheel, torture devices, constancy, heavenly reward, Christian history</keywords>

<term>The Wheel</term> (rad): One of the most feared capital punishments of the era, where a victim was tied to a wagon wheel and their limbs were broken, or the wheel was used to press the body against sharp spikes.
<term>Sledgehammers</term> (mokers): Heavy tools used by executioners to shatter the bones of the condemned, often as a "mercy" to hasten death after prolonged torture on the wheel.

**Vocabulary:** Christians, martyring, wheel, spears, scorching, horrors, glory.

SCENES OF THE <note>The full title is "Tafereelen der Eerste Christenen" or "Scenes of the First Christians."</note>
LI.

## *Fifty-first Scene.*

Hesychius, a hero, who under the Emperor's legions
    Had fought with fame, now lays down his military service,
He strives for defenselessness <note>The Dutch term "weerloosheid" refers to the principle of non-resistance or pacifism, a core value of the Anabaptist tradition from which this book likely originates.</note> in the footsteps of his Lord,
And chooses persecution and misery over earthly rule.

With scorn and insult they send him to the Workhouse <note>original: "Tuchthuis" — a house of correction or prison where inmates were forced to perform manual labor.</note>,
    Where he is given a distaff <note>A tool used in spinning wool; being forced to do "women's work" was intended as a deep humiliation for a former Roman soldier.</note> instead of his spear;
Yet this insult strengthens the hero’s heart all the more
To turn himself to his God in the silence.

O Christian Hercules, who spins among the women,
Whose heart loves no Omphale <note>In Greek mythology, Hercules was enslaved to Queen Omphale and forced to wear women’s clothes and spin wool as a penance.</note>, but your Anointed <note>Christ.</note>,
How far does your virtue exceed the strength of Alcides <note>Another name for Hercules, referring to his lineage from Alcaeus.</note>!

He fought with monsters, you with sins, disaster, and suffering;
He was burned by a woman’s love in the robe of Nessus <note>A poisoned garment that led to the agonizing death of Hercules.</note>,
In vain they drowned you, seeking to extinguish the Divine fire.

<summary>This page depicts the martyrdom of Hesychius, a Roman soldier who renounced his military rank for Christ. It uses a sophisticated parallel between his forced labor at a spinning wheel and the mythological humiliation of Hercules by Omphale.</summary>
<keywords>Hesychius, non-resistance, Hercules, Omphale, Nessus, Roman military, pacifism, spinning, martyrdom</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. LI.
## 51

->*Hesychius placed in the Spinning House because he laid down his military service.*<-

Hesychius must here in the Spinning House <note>original: "Spinhuys" — a house of correction where inmates, usually women, were forced to spin thread as punishment. For a former soldier, this was a specialized form of public humiliation designed to mock his perceived loss of "manly" martial spirit.</note>, to his disgrace,
Perform that labor alongside a row of wicked women,
Because he, enlightened by God’s word, hates military service <note>This reflects the "weerloosheid" or pacifist stance of the early Christians, particularly emphasized in Anabaptist traditions.</note>
And casts it off, to place his trust in God his Creator,
And the Supreme Commander who conquered hell and death.
Thus the darkness gives way before the Gospel-sun.

SCENES OF THE
<meta>visible bleed-through from verso</meta>

### Fifty-second Scene.

A warrior, in the service of Emperor Julian
The Apostate <note>original: "Godtverloochenaar" — literally "God-denier." Julian (r. 361–363 AD) was the last pagan emperor of the Roman Empire; he was called "the Apostate" because he rejected Christianity in favor of Neoplatonic paganism.</note>, we see appearing in the field,
He is welcomed with cheers by his fellow soldiers,
Who cast wondering eyes upon his costly armor.

One sees him go courageously to the commander-in-chief;
He lays his helmet, the sword, and the armor at his feet,
And says: I am content through fasting, prayer, and penance,
Rather than seeking laurels through weapons of war.

I shall follow the banner of the cross of my Savior,
Though the world hates me, and however much you are enraged,
I confess before the whole army <note>original: "heir" — an archaic term for a great military host or army.</note> that I am a Christian.

Oh brave Champion, more praise is given to you
By striving toward heaven with your triumph of the cross,
Than if the Emperor were to recognize you as his brother-in-arms <note>original: "spitsbroêr" — a close comrade or "spear-brother." This term implies a high level of intimacy and shared status with the sovereign.</note>.

->*A soldier, having become a Christian, lays down his weapons <note>original: "geweer" — in this 17th-century context, this term refers generally to a soldier's arms or military equipment, such as his sword and belt, rather than a firearm.</note> in the sight of the whole army 52*<-

# SCENES OF THE
# LIV.

### *Fifty-fourth Scene.*

Observe how these pious ranks <note>original: "Godtvruchte reijen" — referring to the orderly, devout procession of the faithful.</note>
Lay a Christian body to rest,
Not in dark mourning clothes,
As the foolish heathens are accustomed to:
But with a heart inclined toward virtue,
As can be seen upon their faces.

They prefer to sing with joy,
While the body is by the Elders <note>original: "Ouderlingen" — church leaders or presbyters who oversaw the community's spiritual life and rituals.</note>
Carried to an honorable grave,
From where it shall hereafter arise,
On the Last Day <note>original: "Jongsten dage" — literally the "youngest day," a traditional term for the Day of Judgment or the Resurrection.</note>, to praise God,
And cast off all that is mortal.

What use is sighing, weeping, or lamenting,
For one who, released from earthly things,
Awaits the immortality of the soul?
One should rather mourn for the wicked,
Who neglect the favor of heaven,
And live recklessly and without thought.

<summary>This page describes the burial customs of early Christians, contrasting their joyful songs and hope for resurrection with the despairing mourning rituals of the "heathens." It emphasizes that death for a believer is a release into immortality rather than a cause for worldly grief.</summary>
<keywords>Christian burial, afterlife, resurrection, Elders, mourning customs, immortality of the soul, early Church, Jan Luyken</keywords>

SCENES OF THE
LV.

### Fifty-fifth Scene.

**T**iburtius <note>Saint Tiburtius (3rd century AD) was a Christian martyr, said to be the son of the Roman prefect Chromatius. His story is often associated with that of Saint Sebastian.</note>, imprisoned for his faith,
Dragged to the court with scorn by the heathen people,
Shows himself steadfast in his suffering.
He dared courageously to combat idolatry <note>original: "afgodery" — the worship of Roman gods, which Christians viewed as false idols.</note>,
And to declare Jesus' teaching with his mouth
In the place where the strict judge stood,
Who ordered him to walk upon glowing coals of fire,
Which he does, with fiery prayers
To God, his salvation, in whom he trusts,
Who keeps him unharmed in the heat.
Whereupon he says in the sight of the godless:
"This flame smells as lovely to me as roses.
"Come, judge, feel if you too can be freed
"From pain, like I am, by your god Jupiter <note>original: "Jupyn" — an archaic Dutch poetic form of Jupiter, the king of the Roman gods.</note>.
"Come show us now that by beseeching him
"You dare to stick a finger in boiling water."
Tiburtius, illustrious Martyr,
It is in vain to try for that blasphemer,
Who wickedly dared to accuse you of sorcery <note>original: "tovery" — Roman authorities often attributed the miraculous survival of martyrs to "magic" or "sorcery" rather than divine intervention.</note>,
To teach and enlighten him through the miracle;
He remains hardened, and blinded by hate,
While you find your help and refuge in God.
The Christian feels no pain from earthly fire:
The flame of God's love consumes it in his heart.

<term>Tiburtius</term> (An early Christian martyr); <term>Martyr</term> (One who suffers death for their beliefs); <term>Jupiter</term> (The supreme Roman deity); <term>Faith</term> (Sincere belief); <term>Miracle</term> (A supernatural act); <term>Idolatry</term> (The worship of idols).

<summary>This page recounts the trial and miraculous ordeal of Saint Tiburtius, who walked across hot coals unharmed, using the event to challenge the power of the Roman gods and the cruelty of his persecutors.</summary>

FIRST CHRISTIANS. 55.

### Fifty-fifth Scene.

**T**iburtius <note>Saint Tiburtius (3rd century AD) was a Christian martyr, said to be the son of the Roman prefect Chromatius. His story is often associated with that of Saint Sebastian.</note>, imprisoned for his faith,
Dragged to the court with scorn by the heathen people,
Shows himself steadfast in his suffering.
He dared courageously to combat idolatry <note>original: "afgodery" — the worship of Roman gods, which Christians viewed as false idols.</note>,
And to declare Jesus' teaching with his mouth
In the place where the strict judge stood,
Who ordered him to walk upon glowing coals of fire,
Which he does, with fiery prayers
To God, his salvation, in whom he trusts,
Who keeps him unharmed in the heat.
Whereupon he says in the sight of the godless:
"This flame smells as lovely to me as roses.
"Come, judge, feel if you too can be freed
"From pain, like I am, by your god Jupiter <note>original: "Jupyn" — an archaic Dutch poetic form of Jupiter, the king of the Roman gods.</note>.
"Come show us now that by beseeching him
"You dare to stick a finger in boiling water."
Tiburtius, illustrious Martyr,
It is in vain to try for that blasphemer,
Who wickedly dared to accuse you of sorcery <note>original: "tovery" — Roman authorities often attributed the miraculous survival of martyrs to "magic" or "sorcery" rather than divine intervention.</note>,
To teach and enlighten him through the miracle;
He remains hardened, and blinded by hate,
While you find your help and refuge in God.
The Christian feels no pain from earthly fire:
The flame of God's love consumes it in his heart.

->Tiburtius walking upon glowing coals<-

> What! Does Tiburtius tread on coals? No, oh no!
> They are transformed into refreshing roses,
> Upon which he can step unharmed through Heaven’s help.
> Thus the Almighty knows, before the eyes of the godless,
> How to constantly perform wonders, when it serves His honor,
> Although here it is stained with the name of sorcery.

<meta>This page continues the series of "Scenes" depicting the lives and miracles of early Christians. SCENES OF THE [FIRST CHRISTIANS]
LVII.

## Fifty-seventh Scene.

See here, artfully depicted in this scene <note>original: "tafereel" — a scene, picture, or dramatic representation.</note>,
A matter that antiquity <note>original: "aêloudheid" — referring here to the era of the early Church fathers and the first centuries of Christianity.</note> shares with us.
An old man in the early Christian days,
Who lived according to Heaven's pleasure,
Was often visited in his hut by his friend,
Whose little son, whom he sometimes brought along,
Fell into sickness and subsequently passed away.
The father, after many fiery prayers,
Has laid the body of the child in a basket,
Covered over with the leaves and fruit of a vineyard;
Thereupon he goes to speak to the old man again,
Leaves him his basket, and has returned home.
The gray-haired man wants to reach for the fruit:
But sees the child, upon which he immediately concludes,
That his friend's faith and firm trust
Is nothing other than to behold his son alive again.
He calls upon the Lord for assistance,
Whereupon the child lives within its basket full of leaves.
Where is that power of the first Christian heroes?
Where is the living faith of which they tell us?
Alas! The Pentecostal fire <note>original: "Pinxtervuur" — referring to the fire of the Holy Spirit that descended on the Apostles, symbolizing spiritual zeal and miraculous power.</note> is extinguished daily,
While the mouth says that the heart believes!

<summary>This page recounts a miracle where an early Christian hermit, through prayer and the profound faith of a grieving father, resurrects a child hidden in a basket of grapes. It concludes with a lament that modern faith lacks the "Pentecostal fire" and miraculous power of the early Church.</summary>

<keywords>resurrection, early Christians, faith, Pentecostal fire, miracle, hermit, grieving father</keywords>

<header>SCENES OF THE</header>
<sig>LVIII.</sig>

### *Fifty-eighth Scene.*

A certain youth (according to the account of Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian who recorded numerous contemporary miracles in his writings, particularly in *The City of God*.</note>)
While at play, was run over by a wagon;
He was said to be, at a place named Audurus <note>original: "Audurus" — likely referring to Uzalis, a town near Hippo where a shrine to St. Stephen was famous for miracles recorded by Augustine.</note>,
Thus raised up again through fervent prayers.
The Mother brings her deceased child
In sorrow to the grave of Stephen the Saint <term>Stefanus</term> <note>Saint Stephen, the first Christian martyr, whose relics were believed to possess great healing power in the 5th century.</note>,
And prays to the Lord, whose love she experiences,
That He might restore her son, and keep him safe—
Should he live again—from worldly vanity.
“Your power,” she says, “is as it was of old,
“Your great name is constantly spread by us,
“Your holy light has shone upon our souls;
“To the Apostles You granted the spirit and power
“To draw the nations to You through Your wonders;
“Through the fire of Pentecost <note>original: "Pinxtervuur" — referring to the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the Apostles, granting them the ability to perform miracles.</note>, You gave them might, O Lord!
“To raise the dead in Your name!
“If You will it, my son is saved.
“You do will it, O Lord! I shall praise You eternally!”
Whereupon, through faith, following her prayer,
She sees the youth rise up from the dead.

<vocab>Augustine, Audurus, Stephen, Pentecost fire, Youth, Resurrection, Run over by a wagon</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts a miracle recorded by Saint Augustine, in which a grieving mother’s prayer at the tomb of Saint Stephen restores her son to life after a fatal wagon accident. It emphasizes the continuation of Apostolic power and the triumph of faith over death.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Augustine, Saint Stephen, resurrection, miracle, Audurus, Pentecost, faith, maternal prayer</keywords>

SCENES OF THE
LIX.

### *Fifty-ninth Scene.*

The Christians, by command of Diocletian <note>Roman Emperor (r. 284–305 AD) known for the "Great Persecution" of Christians.</note>,
->Thrown before the wild beasts,<-
Who, though incited against them, remain standing meekly,
->And frolic playfully around them,<-
Lift their minds to heaven in the midst of danger.
->The heathens, driven by spite,<-
Cause a wild bull with a terrible clamor
->To be incited: yet it spares their lives:<-
Neither the glowing irons nor the prods can make the beast
->Rage against the Martyrs,<-
Who with meekness, and under the banner of the Lamb <note>A reference to Jesus Christ, often depicted as a sacrificial lamb.</note>
->Keep their souls untainted,<-
And triumph over wicked Idolatry,
->Which they, through the cross and bitter suffering,<-
With the Angel Michael, God’s general <term>veldheer: a military commander or field marshal</term>, on their side,
->Fight against with full virtue and bravery.<-
If ever a hero earned praise who died for his fatherland,
->He shall gain much greater praise,<-
Who in the spiritual struggle overcomes sin
->So as never to be deprived of the face of God.<-
Steadfast Martyrs in the arena of Tyre <note>An ancient Phoenician city where many Christians were martyred in the arena.</note>,
->The hellish beasts must yield.<-
The shield of patience <term>lydzaamheid: long-suffering or patient endurance</term> makes you strong in the struggle.
->You shall receive the heavenly palm branch <note>A traditional symbol of victory and martyrdom.</note>.<-

<summary>This page describes the miraculous survival of Christian martyrs in the arena of Tyre during the persecutions of Emperor Diocletian, where wild animals refused to attack them. The text emphasizes spiritual victory over physical suffering and compares the martyr's sacrifice to that of a national hero.</summary>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. LIX.
59

->*A wild bull in the arena at Tyre, incited in vain with glowing prods against the Martyrs.*<-

Unhappy Tyrians <note>The inhabitants of Tyre, an ancient Phoenician port city in modern-day Lebanon.</note>, who follow
Your Prince Diocletian <note>Gaius Aurelius Valerius Diocletianus (r. 284–305 AD), the Roman Emperor who launched the final and most severe persecution of Christians in the Roman Empire.</note> in cruelty, and place
These Martyrs in the arena <term>schouwperk: literally "spectacle-ground," referring to the Roman amphitheater used for public games and executions</term>; feel free to incite
The terrible beast with hot prods: but, oh raging barbarians!
It is more compassionate than you. Ah! Do you not notice
That such a miracle is performed by the Almighty?

P 3
<meta>catchword: Six-</meta>

<summary>This page features a poetic reflection on a miraculous event in the arena of Tyre, where a wild bull refuses to attack Christian martyrs despite being provoked with red-hot irons by the Roman authorities. It contrasts the natural mercy of the beast with the inhuman cruelty of the persecutors, attributing the animal's restraint to divine intervention.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic reflection on the martyrdoms in the Roman arena. SCENES OF THE
LX.

### Sixtieth Scene.

How can you, with your bloody sword,
Stubborn paganism <note>The author uses "heidendom" to refer to the Roman polytheistic authorities who viewed the growth of Christianity as a threat to the state.</note>, thus rage against God’s saints?
They strive for no power on earth.
You need not protect the Emperors in this manner.
They do not seek a crown through rebellion.
Their **spiritual sword** <note>A reference to the "Sword of the Spirit" from the New Testament (Ephesians 6:17), signifying the word of God and faith rather than physical weapons.</note> battles the world, the flesh, and sins,
With Jesus, God’s chosen son,
To whose dominion they have bound their soul.
Stop! For they love you.
They will pray for your salvation to the **Supreme Savior**.
Lay down the sword, and be not afraid;
The tamer of the wild, their King, hovers in the midst.
But alas! You are not convinced,
And are much more cruel than these hungry animals.
The lion, the bear, the tiger bow:
But your bloodlust wants to let loose the hellish reins.
Alas! There you plunge your sword into the body
Of the **Martyrs**, while they bow before their Savior.
God’s punishment approaches you for the crime,
And everyone whom you kill awakens a thousand **blood-witnesses** <term>bloedgetuigen: the literal Dutch translation of the Greek word 'martys' (martyr), meaning one who bears witness through their death</term>.

<vocab>paganism, God’s saints, spiritual sword, Supreme Savior, Martyrs, blood-witnesses</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the scene of the Tyrian martyrs by contrasting the miraculous gentleness of the arena's beasts with the relentless violence of the Roman executioners. It warns the persecutors that killing the faithful only serves to inspire thousands more to join the Christian cause.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, Roman Empire, religious persecution, spiritual warfare, Jan Luyken, Tyre, divine protection, blood-witnesses</keywords>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS. LX.

60

<meta>Catchword: One-</meta>

<image-desc>An etching depicting the martyrdom of Christians in a Roman amphitheater. In the center foreground, Roman soldiers armed with swords and wearing helmets attack a group of unarmed men; some of the victims are kneeling in prayer, while others have fallen to the ground. A large crowd of spectators is seated in the surrounding tiers of the arena. In the background, classical Roman architecture including a domed building and a tall column can be seen. ->The Martyrs in the Arena put to death by the sword<-

You have indeed escaped the sharp teeth of the animals,
oh Christian company! <note>The original "Christen-rei" refers to a choir or a gathered group of believers.</note> but not the all-destroying sword
Of men, who are to be feared much more than beasts,
Since their sharpened ingenuity, which pairs with their cruelty,
Devises the fiercest pain: thus their malice <note>The Dutch word "spijt" here implies a sense of spiteful resentment or grudge-driven malice.</note> causes you to perish:
Yet the fate of death is for you a path to the blessed life.

<term>Martyrs</term>: from the Greek *martys*, meaning witnesses who testify to their faith through death.
<term>Arena</term>: <note>original: "Schouwperk"</note> literally a "show-pen" or theater, referring to the Roman amphitheaters where public executions were held as entertainment.
<term>Christian company</term>: a poetic way of describing the martyrs as a unified group, often likened to a liturgical choir.
<term>Jan Luyken</term>: (1649–1712) a famous Dutch poet, illustrator, and engraver known for his vivid and often gruesome depictions of religious martyrdom.
<term>Persecution</term>: the systematic mistreatment of the early Christians by the Roman State.
<term>Sword</term>: the standard military weapon used by Roman soldiers for execution when wild animals failed to kill the condemned.
<term>Cruelty</term>: the "sharpened ingenuity" mentioned refers to the human capacity to invent tortures beyond the natural instincts of predatory animals.

<summary>This page features an engraving by Jan Luyken depicting Christians being executed by Roman soldiers in an arena, accompanied by a poem that argues human cruelty is more inventive and fearsome than that of wild beasts.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, Roman Empire, Jan Luyken, persecution, arena, execution, Christian history, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

**Vocabulary**

<term>Sixtieth Scene</term>: The specific sequence number in Jan Luyken's 17th-century work.
<term>Paganism</term>: <note>original: "heidendom"</note> Here used specifically to describe the Roman authorities and citizens who rejected the Christian God.
<term>Supreme Savior</term>: <note>original: "Opperheiland"</note> A respectful Dutch title for Jesus.

<summary>This page contains a moralizing poem that rebukes the Roman persecutors for their cruelty, contrasting the Christians' peaceful spiritual nature with the violent "bloodlust" of the state. It frames the execution of martyrs not as a defeat, but as a catalyst that creates thousands more "blood-witnesses" for the faith.</summary>

LXI.
<header>S C E N E S  O F  T H E</header>

### Sixty-first Scene.

Jerome, <note>original: "Hironimus" — Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a prominent Church Father and scholar best known for his Latin translation of the Bible.</note> who in former times
Did write the life of Hilarion, <note>Saint Hilarion the Great (291–371 AD) was an anchorite who lived much of his life in the desert and is credited with introducing monasticism to Palestine.</note>
Relates that which this print shows;
How one Orionus, possessed
By many spirits, and in a chain
Confined, spared neither friend nor kinsman. <note>original: "maag" — an archaic Dutch term for a blood relative or family member.</note>
Hilarion knew, according to his <insert>Jerome’s</insert> writing,
How to drive those spirits out of his body.

He keeps himself calm and safe,
Even though this Orionus lifts the saint
With great strength from the ground.
The bystanders cry out, and are terrified,
And fear that he will suffocate:
But no, he opens his mouth,
Makes all the spirits depart, <note>original: "heenevaaren" — to travel away or depart; in this context, the departure of demons.</note>
And calms that tormented <note>original: "bestreden" — literally "battled" or "contested," referring to the man's internal struggle with the spirits.</note> man.

**Vocabulary**

<term>Jerome</term> (Hironimus), <term>Hilarion</term>, <term>Orionus</term>, <term>spirits</term> (geesten), <term>chain</term> (keten), <term>print</term> (prent), <term>scene</term> (tafereel), <term>sixty-first</term> (eenenzeftigste)

<summary>This page describes a scene where Saint Hilarion exorcises a legion of spirits from a man named Orionus, a story originally recorded by Saint Jerome. It emphasizes the saint's calm spiritual authority in the face of violent physical possession.</summary>

# THE FIRST CHRISTIANS. LXI.

->A possessed man lifts Hilarion high from the ground.<-

Hilarion, gifted at driving out spirits, <note>Hilarion the Great (c. 291–371 AD) was a celebrated desert hermit and healer in the early Christian tradition.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Is lifted from the ground with great force,
By a possessed man overcome by those plagues: <note>original: "plaagen" — plagues or afflictions, here referring to the man's demonic possession.</note>

Alas! How many are possessed by abominations,
Who do not wish to acknowledge their ailment, however sharply felt. <note>The poet Jan Luyken often concludes his verses with a moral application, suggesting that hidden sins are a form of spiritual "possession" that many people refuse to recognize.</note>

SCENES OF THE
3

### Sixty-second Scene.

° Athanasius, is it true <note>Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD) was the Bishop of Alexandria and the author of the *Life of Saint Anthony*, which popularized the stories of the desert hermits.</note>
That your pen has in his life story
Described the old Desert Dweller? <note>original: "Woestyniers" — referring to Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356 AD), a pioneer of Christian monasticism in the Egyptian desert.</note>
To me, it seems wondrous and strange.
Did Anthony have to, every day,
Let himself be plagued by evil spirits?

Can you believe that a man
Who seeks to fear his Savior, <note>In this context, "to fear" means to hold in holy awe or deep reverence.</note>
Could be so plagued by ghosts,
And that he could send the Devil’s cursed crew,
By making a small sign of the cross, <note>original: "kruiſkens maaken" — the ritual gesture of the sign of the cross, often used in hagiography as a weapon against demons.</note>
To the Abyss, the realm of the soul’s misery?

I rather think that by the spiritual cross
Of his Savior, borne with faithfulness,
He can drive away, as if they were evil spirits,
The internal, sinful rabble;
From which he, through steadfast struggle
And watchfulness, must free his heart. <note>The poet, likely Jan Luyken, interprets the famous "Temptations of Saint Anthony" not as literal physical attacks by demons, but as metaphors for the internal struggle against worldly desires and sins.</note>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS.
## LXII.

<image-desc>An engraving by Jan Luyken. On the left, a supernatural creature with the head of a donkey, a human torso girded with leaves, and animalistic legs stands on a path. ->*Anthony, at the appearance of a ghost, marks himself with a cross on the forehead. J. Luyken 62*<-

->The pious Anthony makes the specter yield here,<-
->Because his forehead is marked with a cross.<-
->Yet a deluded spirit often dwells within him,<- <note>The poet suggests that the "ghosts" Anthony encountered might be the result of a mind "dazed" by isolation and intense meditation.</note>
->Which, through much thinking, dreams of strange night-visions,<-
->Whereby the imagination fills the brain with delusion and appearance;<-
->Although this example could well be pure truth.<- <note>A characteristic 17th-century reflection, balancing rational skepticism about "imagination" with a traditional belief in the reality of spiritual combat.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the accounts of Saint Athanasius to the story of the North African martyr Saint Perpetua. ->SCENES OF THE [CHURCH]<- <note>The full title is likely "Tafereelen der Christelijke Martelaaren" (Scenes of the Christian Martyrs).</note>
->LXIV.<-

### Sixty-fourth Scene.

Perpetua, the heroine of the cross,
In an ecstasy of the senses, <note>This refers to a divine vision or "verrukking" experienced by the saint during her imprisonment in 203 AD.</note>
Saw a golden ladder standing,
Planted with knives, swords, and blades,
Through which she sees Satyrus <note>Satyrus was Perpetua's mentor and fellow prisoner; in her vision, he climbed the ladder first to encourage her.</note> pressing,
And climbing up to go to Heaven.

He calls to her: you must follow us:
Though the dragon <note>In the original account, a "huge dragon" or serpent lay at the foot of the ladder, attempting to frighten the martyrs away.</note> appears enraged at you,
You can thus flee his cruelty;
Upon this, she immediately decided,
Along with her companions in faith,
Not to shrink from the pains of martyrdom.

She strives through fire, and sword and spears,
To triumph beside her God,
Climbs the ladder of faith,
And follows the pure Martyrs,
Who had climbed up before her.
The dragon of the abyss misses that prey.

<term>Perpetua</term> (a young noblewoman of Carthage), <term>Satyrus</term> (the Christian who converted Perpetua's family), Martyrs, golden ladder, dragon, heroine of the cross, Heaven.

LXIV. 64

<image-desc>An etching by Jan Luyken depicting the vision of Saint Perpetua. ->*Perpetua sees a vision.*<- <note>original: "gezigt." In this context, it refers to a divine revelation or prophetic sight experienced while she was in prison.</note>

Perpetua, what useful lessons we learn from your vision!
The sharp Ladder is the narrow path to life; <note>A reference to the biblical "narrow way" (Matthew 7:14), here visualized as a ladder guarded by weapons to symbolize the trials of faith.</note>
The Dragon represents the enemies who from honor and duty
Distance us, to strive along the path of sins;
The one who courageously climbs is an Example and God’s word,
Through which we are encouraged toward <term>piety</term> (original: *godsvrucht*, meaning a life of deep devotion and religious duty).

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's account of Saint Perpetua's vision of the ladder, this page transitions to the visions experienced by the martyrs Agapius and James (Jacobus) while they were imprisoned in North Africa during the Valerian persecutions (circa 259 AD).</meta>

SCENES OF THE

## *Sixty-fifth Scene.*

Agapius, who in the dungeon
Prayed for himself and two Young Women
To his God, his refuge and strength,
Sees a Youth approach him,
Who had suffered for the pure faith
And had won the joy of Heaven.
<note>Agapius was a contemporary of Saint Cyprian. He was imprisoned with the deacon James (Jacobus) and the reader Marianus.</note>

He held a palm branch in his hands,
And a wreath of roses, which seemed to glow
Around his neck like rubies;
And spoke: cast away all your cares,
Rejoice: for tomorrow you shall be
With me at <term>Sion's wedding</term> <note>original: *Sions bruiloft*. A common biblical metaphor for the banquet in Heaven where the soul is united with Christ.</note>.

It is said that when James mourned,
Something similar happened to him:
He thought he saw a Youth
Clad in white floating around him,
Who gave him two girdles,
Upon which lay a glow of purple.
<note>James (Jacobus) and Agapius are often commemorated together; their hagiography includes several vivid dreams that encouraged them to face the arena.</note>

This one says to him and his friends,
Who find themselves in the dungeon:
Follow me quickly: and after that day
This strengthened them in their struggle;
So God comforts those who confess Him,
With humility, love, and deep awe.

SCENES OF THE

### Sixty-sixth Scene.

Did Emperor *Constantine* see this sign in the sky?
->Or is it a fabrication born of a desire<-
For supreme rule, to blind everyone,
->And to find a stronger arm;<-
<note>The "stronger arm" refers to the growing political and military support Constantine could gain by aligning with the Christians.</note>
Since *Christendom* grew in wealth and power,
->And earthly pomp and splendor<-
Had captured the hearts of the heads of the church?
->He sought to tame *Maxentius*,<-
<note>Maxentius was Constantine's rival and the fellow-emperor whom he defeated at the Battle of the Milvian Bridge.</note>
And found no chance, unless he called himself a *Christian*
->And condemned paganism.<-
Did there stand in the sky by the Cross: IN THIS YOU SHALL CONQUER,
<note>original: "IN DIT ZULT GE OVERWINNEN". This is the Dutch translation of the famous Latin phrase *In hoc signo vinces*, which Constantine reportedly saw in a vision.</note>
->While he until then his senses,<-
As befits a *Christian*, did not bend to God's word:
->But lived in vengeance and murder?<-
Would *Christ* give this miraculous sign to a prince,
->Who at the end of his life<-
Scarcely received baptism—indeed, had first refused it—
->And had lived a life of crude excess?<-
*Eusebius*, you certainly contradict yourself,
<note>Eusebius of Caesarea was a contemporary bishop and historian who wrote a famous, highly favorable biography of Constantine.</note>
->Since you, well-disposed toward that prince,<-
Flatter him, and first write: "He slept when he saw this";
->After that you say: "It was day,<-
"And in the sight of the army, as the prince with oaths
->"Has himself confessed to me."<-
That artistic fabrication, O *Bishop*, lacks its support.
->That oath was entirely useless:<-
For if the whole army saw it, why would he need to swear it to you?
->Can you convert the people this way?<-
No, superstition does not please the Deity
->Who searches the heart and the mind.<-
<note>original: "hart en nier doorziet". A biblical idiom meaning "sees through the heart and kidneys," referring to God's ability to see a person's innermost thoughts and true character.</note>

### Sixty-sixth Scene.

Did Emperor Constantine see this sign in the sky?
->Or is it fabricated from a craving<-
For supreme power, to blind everyone,
->And find a stronger arm;<-

->And earthly pomp and splendor<-

->And doomed paganism.<-
Stood in the sky by the Cross: IN THIS YOU SHALL CONQUER,
<note>original: "IN DIT ZULT GE OVERWINNEN". This is the Dutch translation of the famous Latin phrase *In hoc signo vinces*.</note>
->While he, until then, had not yet turned his mind<-
Toward God's word, as befits a Christian:
->But lived in vengeance and murder?<-
<note>The author is highlighting Constantine's violent reputation, including the execution of his own son Crispus and wife Fausta, as contradictory to a divine vision.</note>
Would Christ give this miraculous sign to a prince,
->Who only at the end of his life<-
Barely received baptism—indeed, had first refused it—
->And broke out in rough excess?<-
<note>Constantine famously delayed his baptism until he was on his deathbed, a common but debated practice at the time.</note>
Eusebius, you certainly contradict yourself,
<note>Eusebius of Caesarea was a bishop and historian who wrote the biography of Constantine and recorded the story of the vision.</note>

->"Has confessed to me under oath."<-

For if the whole army saw it, why would he need to swear it to you?
->Is this how you can convert the people?<-
No, superstition does not please the Divinity
->Who sees through heart and kidney.<-
<note>A biblical idiom meaning God knows the most secret thoughts and truest intentions of a person.</note>

# EARLY CHRISTIANS. LXVI.

<note>Jan Luyken (1649–1712) was a prolific Dutch engraver and poet. This work is part of a series illustrating the history and perceived spiritual decline of the early Christian church.</note>

->*Constantine the Great sees a Cross in the Sky.*<-

If it is true, *Constantine*, that the cross appeared to you,
It was not so that you should fight with weapons through it
And conquer, but with tears and prayers.
The kingdom of heaven is indeed conquered, but through suffering.
But alas! you brought the world into the church early on;
Then wealth and the desire for status overcame virtue.
<note>The poet suggests that Constantine's legal recognition of Christianity (the "Peace of the Church") led to "secularization," where the humble, persecuted faith became corrupted by the power and riches of the Roman Empire.</note>

<meta>catchword: Ze-</meta>

SCENES OF THE

LXVII.

### *Sixty-seventh Scene.*

Thessalonica melts in tears:
As it sees nine thousand <term>Arians<gloss>Followers of Arius, who held a specific theological view on the nature of Christ that was declared heretical by the mainstream church.</gloss></term>
Fall by the murderous sword.
Are these Christians who, so enraged,
Persecute their opponents out of revenge,
Or men who have strayed from virtue?

Surely: one cannot with steel blades
Force the wavering mind,
And it especially does not suit a Christian,
Who must work for the conversion of souls
By the power of pure teaching,
As the holy word commands him.

It was, <term>Theodosius<gloss>Theodosius I (r. 379–395 AD), the Roman Emperor who made Nicene Christianity the official state religion.</gloss></term>, a sign
That you had turned away from God
When you committed that cruel murder;
<note>This refers to the Massacre of Thessalonica in 390 AD, where the Emperor ordered the execution of thousands of citizens in retaliation for the killing of a Roman official.</note>
It has left an indelible stain
Upon your illustrious life,
Yes, it has wounded Christ’s bride, the church.

<summary>This page features a poetic critique of Emperor Theodosius I for the Massacre of Thessalonica, arguing that religious belief cannot be forced by the sword. The poet laments how state-sanctioned violence against "Arians" stained the character of both the Emperor and the Christian church.</summary>
<keywords>Theodosius I, Thessalonica, Arians, religious violence, coercion, Jan Luyken, Church history, persecution</keywords>

->The <term>Emperor Theodosius<gloss>Theodosius I (r. 379–395 AD), the last emperor to rule both the Eastern and Western Roman Empire, known for making Nicene Christianity the state religion.</gloss></term> causes 7,000 people to be put to death at <term>Thessalonica<gloss>A major city in Macedonia (modern-day Greece) where a riot in 390 AD led to the murder of a Roman official, sparking the Emperor's lethal retaliation.</gloss></term>.<-

<meta>This page continues the moral and historical critique of Emperor Theodosius I.
