SCENES OF THE

### *Sixty-eighth Scene.*

Stand: do not seek to enter the Temple,
As one of Christ’s precious members,
    ô Emperor <term>Theodosius<gloss>Theodosius I (r. 379–395 AD), the last emperor to rule over both the Eastern and Western halves of the Roman Empire.</gloss></term>!
How shall the Bishop receive you,
With God-sanctified songs,
    And a Christian kiss?

<term>Ambrose<gloss>Saint Ambrose (c. 339–397 AD), the influential Bishop of Milan who famously barred the Emperor from the cathedral until he performed public penance for his role in the Massacre of Thessalonica.</gloss></term> shall make you turn back;
You would desecrate the temple choir;
    Because you are stained with the blood
Of nine thousand <term>Arians<gloss>Followers of Arianism, a theological doctrine often persecuted as heresy; while the historical massacre at Thessalonica targeted the general citizenry, the poet here emphasizes the blood of religious dissidents.</gloss></term>.
Smelt in your tears for that crime,
    And fall like <term>David<gloss>The biblical King David, who famously repented before God for his sins of adultery and murder.</gloss></term> at God’s feet.

How can those who fear God,
Be fellow members with persecutors?
    If they differ from your church,
Seek to convince them through teaching.
The sword can bend no one’s heart:
    For that is the work of Heaven alone.

SCENES OF THE
LXX.

### *Seventieth Scene.*

Could then Honorius, the Emperor, within Rome
    Not restrain the wild and fickle commoners,
Unless he permitted and watched the combat in the arena, <term>kampgevecht: gladiatorial combat or hand-to-hand struggle</term> body to body? Oh, wicked deed!
Did he, who was a Christian, take pleasure that noble souls
    Fell unnecessarily by the broadsword?
Surely, then the church was not yet out of its slavery,
    Nor he free from the pagan life;
Then he set the door open for all horrors,
    Which crept into the Christian temples.
Telemachus, that old and pious Christian man,
    Is a martyr <term>bloedgetuige: literally a "blood-witness," the traditional Dutch term for a martyr</term> of this,
Who, without regard for the Imperial power,
    Full of courage entered the fighting-pit,
Separated the champions, and spoke as a Christian;
    Which so ignited the people in wrath,
That he, who had softened the hearts of the combatants,
    Was stoned to death.
This incident <note>Saint Telemachus was a monk who, according to the historian Theodoret, sought to stop the gladiatorial fights in Rome in 404 AD. His death led Emperor Honorius to issue a ban on the practice.</note> has so touched the heart of the Emperor,
    That such combat has since been abolished.
Ah! if this spectacle were considered by the princes,
    What blood shed in the wars,
Out of glory or self-interest, would have been spared in Europe!
    The law of virtue would then have conquered the sword.

<summary>This text recounts the martyrdom of Saint Telemachus, whose intervention in a Roman gladiatorial arena led Emperor Honorius to finally abolish the games. The author uses the event to critique the senseless bloodshed of European wars driven by royal ambition.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Honorius, Saint Telemachus, Rome, gladiatorial games, martyrdom, pacifism, Christian history, abolition of combat</keywords>

The Christian lambs transform here into lions:
Telemachus is murdered for his good counsel,

Freedom is abused, for which the pious soul weeps.

### Seventy-Second Scene.

Oh, Symmachus, what possesses you!
Can you not reach the Bishop’s throne <note>original: "'s Biſſchops ſtoel," referring to the Papal See in Rome.</note>
Without stirring up a hellish riot?
Is this the example the Apostles set?
And did one see them, through schemes and factions, <term>schemes and factions: translates "kuiperyën," referring to political intrigue or underhanded maneuvering for power.</term>
Fighting one another for higher rank?

And you, proud Lawrence, <note>Laurentius (d. 506) was an archpriest elected as a rival Pope (Antipope) against Symmachus. Their competing claims led to years of violent street fighting and legal battles in Rome.</note>
Can you be a Christian shepherd,
When everyone must fear your hatred,
As they would a proud and bloodthirsty man?
Will you rule the lambs of God,
O bloody wolf in sheep’s clothing?

Christian, learn from this calamity,
Brought about within Rome,
To restrain such wicked scheming;
And keep watch, so that it may please God
To protect the Church from discord—
The hellish bloodlust of the hypocrites. <note>original: "ſchynheiligen," literally "shining-holies" or those who appear holy on the outside but are not.</note>

<summary>This page contains a moralizing poem regarding the Laurentian Schism, criticizing the violent and political power struggles between Symmachus and Laurentius for the papacy. It serves as a warning against the "wolf-like" nature of leaders who prioritize rank and factionalism over the peace of the Church.</summary>
<keywords>Symmachus, Laurentius, Laurentian Schism, Rome, Papacy, corruption, religious discord, hypocrites</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the internal decay of the early Roman Church, specifically focusing on the Laurentian Schism (c. 498–506 AD). While the previous page likely introduced the legal and ecclesiastical arguments of the conflict, this page presents a visual and poetic condemnation of the violence that erupted in the streets of Rome between the supporters of the rival popes Symmachus and Laurentius.</meta>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS.
LXXII.
<meta>catchword: Three-</meta>

*Symmachus and Laurentius, each desiring to be Bishop, incite the people to murder. 72*

> O Symmachus, and you Laurentius! What mockery <note>original: "hoon," implying a shameful insult or an act that brings public disgrace upon the faith.</note>
> Do you inflict upon your Savior through this inhuman raging!
> Is the sword a shepherd’s crook <term>shepherd's crook: translates "herderstaf" (crosier). A ceremonial staff held by a bishop to symbolize his role as a spiritual shepherd; the poet here contrasts this peaceful symbol with the "sword" of street violence.</term> to guard the people through discipline?
> No Emperors have ever fought so for their crown,
> As you have for the Bishop’s office. <note>original: "Bisschops-ampt," referring to the Papal See in Rome.</note> Alas! It is lost, <note>original: "omgekomen," meaning perished or destroyed, suggesting the spiritual integrity of the office has died through this violence.</note>
> Unless God raises an arm to restrain you at last.

<summary>This page illustrates the "Laurentian Schism," a violent 5th-century power struggle for the papacy between Symmachus and Laurentius. Through an etching and moralizing poem, it condemns the use of weapons and political ambition within the Church, comparing the bishops' greed unfavorably to that of secular emperors.</summary>

<keywords>Laurentian Schism, Pope Symmachus, Laurentius, Rome, Papal history, religious violence, Jan Luyken, Bishopric, shepherd's crook</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the chronological exploration of Church history, shifting from the Laurentian Schism in Rome (Page 72) to the earlier religious and political turmoil in 5th-century Alexandria under Bishop Cyril. While the previous page critiqued the Roman Papacy, this page addresses the corruption of episcopal power in the Greek East.</meta>

# SCENES OF THE
LXXIII.

### Seventy-third Scene.

Cyril, who, arrogant and bold, 
As Bishop in the Alexandrian seat, 
Followed the tracks of his predecessor Theophilus, 
And lost the true path of the Christians, 
Had sinned most wickedly against the Jews, 
By furiously killing them in their church. <note>original: "kerk." In this historical context, the poet refers to the synagogues of Alexandria, which were attacked and seized by Cyril’s followers around 414–415 AD.</note>
Orestes, who in the Emperor’s name held the governance 
Of that region, punished that monstrous beast: <note>The poet uses "monstrous beast" (monsterdier) to describe Cyril, reflecting a strong anti-clerical sentiment regarding his use of violence.</note>
But he had to pay too dearly for that severity. 
The monks gathered in great heaps, 
Who constantly insulted him wherever he went; 
Indeed, one of them, like a scoundrel, 
Wounded him with a stone: but was thereafter captured 
And harshly punished: since the Imperial interest 
Did not allow that so-called clergy 
Should escape the capital punishment of offended Majesty <term>offended Majesty: translates "gequetste Majesteit," a Dutch rendering of the Latin "crimen laesae maiestatis" (lèse-majesté). It refers to a crime against the dignity or person of the sovereign or his representatives.</term> 
And lord it over the princes; 
Cyril, nevertheless, lets his ban-curse be heard, 
And names, with a hypocritical church-gesture, 
The scoundrel a Christian Martyr. 
Alas! In what a pit are land and church buried, 
When authority falls to temple-wolves! <note>original: "tempelwolven." A metaphorical term for corrupt or predatory religious leaders who use their spiritual office for violent or worldly ends.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the conflict between Bishop Cyril of Alexandria and the Roman Prefect Orestes, specifically focusing on Cyril's persecution of the Jews and the subsequent monk-led riot that resulted in a monk being executed and then controversially declared a martyr by Cyril.</summary>

<keywords>Cyril of Alexandria, Orestes, Theophilus, Alexandria, persecution of Jews, religious violence, Martyrdom, temple-wolves, lèse-majesté, Church history</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the account of the tensions between Bishop Cyril and the Roman Governor Orestes. While the previous page focused on the public insults against Orestes, this page describes the tragic culmination of this conflict: the brutal murder of the philosopher Hypatia by a mob of Cyril’s supporters.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
T
LXXIV.

### Seventy-fourth Scene.

Who does not shudder at Cyril’s <term>madness</term><note>The original Dutch *raazerny* implies a state of frantic, violent rage.</note>?
  Rightly called a cruel church-harpy;
His faction pulls, full of rage, from the carriage
A woman who had conducted herself piously:
  Hypatia, ingenious and learned,
  Whom all the land honors for her wisdom.
They drag her into a church, where she must die,
After they had flayed her body with shards,
  And then hacked her limb from limb (oh, the shame!)
  By monk-executioners, and threw her into the fire.
Say, murderers of such a precious life,
What evil had she committed in your view?
  Nothing else, than that her wise counsel
  Came to the aid of Orestes, the governor of the land,
Who clearly discovered all your Bishop’s deceit,
A Bishop who brought the church to shame;
  And even if it were true that he was insulted,
  It is patience that adorns a Christian:
Here you show yourselves far worse than barbarians,
Who would still spare a tender woman:
  But you, who dare to do all this out of <term>ambition</term><note>The word *heerzucht* specifically refers to a lust for power or dominance.</note>,
  Assault everything like the scum of the abyss.

<summary>This page describes the brutal martyrdom of Hypatia, a Neoplatonist philosopher in Alexandria, who was murdered by a mob of monks supporting Bishop Cyril due to her political influence over the Governor Orestes.</summary>
<keywords>Hypatia, Cyril of Alexandria, Orestes, martyrdom, religious violence, Alexandria, monks, Neoplatonism, 5th century, Dutch poetry</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page follows the account of the philosopher Hypatia’s brutal murder at the hands of a mob in Alexandria. Having depicted the violent consequences of religious fanaticism, the author now pivots to a scathing moral critique of the corrupt clergy—the "false shepherds"—whose worldly excesses and gluttony stand in stark contrast to the virtues of the early Church.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
LXXV.

### Seventy-fifth Tableau.

Are these the shepherds who lead the sheep
Of Christ along the path
To go toward the promised land?
Or are they the fattened priests of Bel <note>original: "Bels gemeste paapen." Bel refers to the ancient deity Baal; here it is used as a pejorative for idolatrous or corrupt clergy who serve their own appetites.</note>,
Who here overload the body with food and drink,
And bathe in a sea of luxury and hellish lust?

They are false shepherds, without morals,
Wine-swillers, drunkards, hot-tempered <note>original: "heet van long," literally "hot of lung," suggesting a fiery, aggressive nature fueled by excess.</note>,
And devourers of wild game, with refined tongues,
Who clothe themselves in the wool of the lambs,
Night-banqueters, the race of Epicurean <note>Referring to the followers of Epicurus, whose philosophy was often misinterpreted by later critics as purely hedonistic.</note> gluttons,
Fattened pigs in the guise of holy Prelates.

Rightly are such <term>drinking-synods</term><note>A translation of the Dutch "slempfinoden," a mocking term for ecclesiastical meetings that devolved into carousing and overindulgence.</note>,
Wherein lust is nurtured,
And from which even a pagan must recoil,
Forbidden in the early Christian Church.
A shepherd who would faithfully guard God’s lambs,
Must renounce luxury, excess, and the desires of the flesh.

<vocab>shepherds, Christ, priests, Epicurus, Prelates, Christian Church, drinking-synods, wine-swillers</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a poetic condemnation of corrupt and hedonistic clergy, contrasting their gluttony and worldliness with the ascetic and faithful duties of true Christian leaders.</summary>
<keywords>clergy corruption, gluttony, false shepherds, Epicureanism, early Christian Church, morality, religious critique, Jan Luyken</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moral critique of the clergy's decadence introduced on page 75. While the previous page provided a visual and poetic description of a drunken episcopal banquet, this page poses a series of rhetorical questions regarding the spiritual consequences of such leadership, contrasting the "temple-swine" with the sober diligence of the Apostles.</meta>

SCENES OF THE
T
76. (LXXVI.)

### Seventy-sixth Scene.

How fares it with the innocent sheep,
When thus the shepherds sleep so carelessly,
As one observes in this print?
How can these temple-swine <note>original: "tempelzwynen"; a derogatory term for corrupt clergy who defile the church through their appetites.</note>
Appear in their pulpits,
And do the work entrusted to them?

How shall these gluttons,
Who cannot be woken even by drums,
And who wallow in the slime and mire,
Fruitfully extol temperance,
And strike most sharply at excess,
So that virtue might not depart from the heart?

How shall they rebuke the drunkards,
When they belch <note>original: "rispen"; to eruct or belch, specifically from overindulgence in food and drink.</note> half-asleep in the pulpit,
And their faces shine and glow with wine?
The preaching of such Baal-prelates <note>original: "Belsprelaaten"; a portmanteau of "Bel" (the idol Baal) and "prelates," implying they serve their own bellies rather than God.</note>
Will be of very little use to those who know them,
And will be an offense to the virtuous heart.

How greatly do the messengers
Of heaven differ from these preachers!
They spoke with a heavenly power,
They were sober and awake,
Constantly plowed the Lord’s field,
And praised Him by day and night.

<vocab>innocent sheep, shepherds, temple-swine, pulpit, gluttons, slime and mire, temperance, virtue, Baal-prelates, preachers, the Lord’s field</vocab>

<summary>This page uses rhetorical verse to condemn clergy who preach virtue while living in gluttony and drunkenness. It concludes by contrasting these "careless shepherds" with the sober and tireless work of the original Apostles.</summary>
<keywords>hypocrisy, clergy, temperance, gluttony, Jan Luyken, shepherds, pulpit, Apostles, sobriety</keywords>

# THE FIRST CHRISTIANS. 76. (LXXVI.)

->Self-indulgent Bishops sleeping off their drunkenness by day.<-

Had Elagabalus <note>Heliogabalus (r. 218–222 AD) was a Roman Emperor infamous for his extreme decadence, eccentricity, and disregard for religious and social norms. The author suggests these "Christian" leaders are even more corrupt than the most notorious pagan.</note> been your contemporary,
He could well have learned revelry from you,
And how one can turn the day into a night,
Oh Priests! Do you not fear Him whom all things fear?
And can you still sleep so carelessly in your sins?
If the Shepherd <note>original: "Herder"; a common biblical metaphor where the clergy are shepherds and the laity are the flock. If the leader is lost, the people are in peril.</note> thus wanders, what becomes of the sheep?

->S C E N E S &nbsp; O F &nbsp; T H E<-
77. (LXXVII.)

### Seventy-seventh Scene.

Emperor Charles, of that name
&nbsp;&nbsp;The fourth <note>Charles IV (1316–1378), Holy Roman Emperor, was known for his efforts to reform the church and state, and for issuing the Golden Bull of 1356.</note>, called his counselors together,
Among whom were many clergymen,
&nbsp;&nbsp;He sees standing by them a Canon <note>original: "Domheer"; a senior member of the clergy attached to a cathedral.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Whose splendor seemed to trouble his heart,
Whose hat he takes from off his hair:

&nbsp;&nbsp;That wild ornament full of ribbon and gold,
&nbsp;&nbsp;He sets upon his head, and says: behold
Whether I now seem Soldier or Canon!
&nbsp;&nbsp;This insult touches not him alone:
&nbsp;&nbsp;But all those who trample upon virtue,
And give offense by their wicked conduct <note>original: "omkeer"; literally a turning or conversion, here used ironically to mean a turning away from the path of righteousness.</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;But could, illustrious prince, this deed,
&nbsp;&nbsp;Performed in your full Council,
Prevent the splendor of the clergy?
&nbsp;&nbsp;Not at all: pride becomes too strong
&nbsp;&nbsp;If it can sneak into the church;
Woe! to him who dares to oppose it.

<term>Emperor Charles</term> (Keizer Karel), <term>Canon</term> (Domheer), <term>Council</term> (Raad), <term>virtue</term> (deugd), <term>pride</term> (hoogmoed), <term>Scene</term> (Tafereel)

<summary>This page recounts an anecdote where Emperor Charles IV shames a Canon for his extravagant, soldier-like attire by wearing the man's golden hat himself. It serves as a warning that pride and worldly vanity have become so deeply rooted in the church that even imperial mockery cannot easily uproot them.</summary>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of moral and historical reflections on the corruption of the clergy. While the previous entries (76 and 77) criticized the gluttony and vanity of the medieval church, this page (79) focuses on the "spirit of persecution" and the use of state military force to enforce religious conformity, specifically citing the 5th-century Bishop Dioscorus and the contemporary practice of using dragoons.</meta>

->SCENES OF THE<-
V
LXXIX.

### *Seventy-ninth Scene.*

Wretched is the land in which the clergy <note>original: "geestlykheid"</note>,
Assisted by the sword of worldly authorities,
Seeks out of envy to trample the neck of innocence,
Because she does not flatter them in their wicked abominations.

How foully a Prince tarnishes his fame and Majesty
Who lends his ears to their hypocritical prayers,
Whereby they gain the footing to force the precious members of Prince Jesus
To follow their will, through steel and brute force <note>original: "onbescheid"; refers to a lack of reason or restraint, often implying injustice.</note>.

Bishop Dioscorus <note>Dioscorus I of Alexandria (died 454 AD). He is infamous in Western church history for the "Robber Council" of Ephesus (449), where he used military presence and threats to force bishops to sign a blank paper and depose his theological rivals.</note>, surrounded by soldiers,
Forced his brothers, who possessed more virtue than he,
To vote in a matter that conflicted with their conscience.

Ah! if Europe were freed from such temple lords <note>original: "tempelheeren"; a derogatory term for high-ranking, powerful clergy.</note>
Who imagine they can convert everyone by means of dragoons <note>original: "dragonders." This is a likely reference to the "Dragonnades" under Louis XIV, where Protestant households were forced to house unruly soldiers until the inhabitants converted to Catholicism.</note>,
How the true church would be refreshed in the midst of adversity!

<vocab>Bishop Dioscorus, Europe, temple lords, dragoons, clergy</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques the use of state military power by the clergy to enforce religious dogmas, using the historical example of Bishop Dioscorus of Alexandria. It concludes with a contemporary lament regarding the use of "dragoons" to force religious conversion in Europe.</summary>
<keywords>clerical corruption, Bishop Dioscorus, religious persecution, dragoons, state and church, Ephesus Council</keywords>

LXXIX.

### *Seventy-ninth Scene.*

Wretched is the land in which the clergy <note>original: "geestlykheid"</note>,
Assisted by the sword of worldly authorities,
Seeks out of envy to trample the neck of innocence,
Because she does not flatter them in their wicked abominations.

How foully a Prince tarnishes his fame and Majesty
Who lends his ears to their hypocritical prayers,
Whereby they gain the footing to force the precious members of Prince Jesus
To follow their will, through steel and brute force <note>original: "onbescheid"; refers to a lack of reason or restraint, often implying injustice or excessive force.</note>.

Bishop Dioscorus <note>Dioscorus I of Alexandria (died 454 AD). He is infamous in Western church history for the "Robber Council" of Ephesus (449), where he used military presence and threats to force bishops to sign a blank paper and depose his theological rivals.</note>, surrounded by soldiers,
Forced his brothers, who possessed more virtue than he,
To vote in a matter that conflicted with their conscience.

Ah! if Europe were freed from such temple lords <note>original: "tempelheeren"; a derogatory term for high-ranking, powerful clergy who prioritized power over faith.</note>
Who imagine they can convert everyone by means of dragoons <note>original: "dragonders." This likely refers to the "Dragonnades" under Louis XIV in the late 17th century, where Protestant households were forced to house unruly soldiers until they converted to Catholicism.</note>,
How the true church would be refreshed in the midst of adversity!

SCENES OF THE
LXXX.

### Eightieth Scene.

O temple-wolf <note>original: "Tempelwolf"; a metaphor for a corrupt or predatory religious leader who devours his flock from within.</note>, arrogant Dioscorus <note>Dioscorus I, Bishop of Alexandria (died 454 AD), notorious for his violent conduct at the Second Council of Ephesus.</note>,
How far you wander from the Christian path!
Do you dare profess the name of the Heavenly Prince <note>original: "Hemelvorst"; referring to Jesus Christ.</note>,
And fight heretics with your false mouth,
While you yourself are a wretched villain,
And with your power fight for the kingdom of the abyss?
Do you dare so wickedly to chain your fellow brothers,
And even thrive upon their ruin and adversity;
Because you are strengthened with earthly power,
While they are despised by the wicked,
Because their voices in church assemblies <note>original: "kerkvergaderingen"; specifically referring to the "Robber Council" of 449 AD where Dioscorus used threats to dictate doctrine.</note>
Cannot be forced to your will or your decree?
But though you lean upon the sword of the authorities,
Tremble before the power of Heavenly Majesty!
God’s Angel shall bind you in chains,
And strike your conscience with eternal lightning,
Along with those whom you misled with your tongue,
When you forced yourself into their council's decrees.
Then those whom you dared so wickedly to seize
Shall approach the Heavenly Prince with holy songs,
Before the diamond throne,
And shine with a star-lit crown.
Hypocrisy <note>original: "Schynheiligheid"</note> may freely enjoy the fat of the earth;
Sincerity <note>original: "Oprechtheid"</note> wins a treasure of much greater value.

SCENES OF THE

## *Eighty-first Scene.*

The Christian who fears and honors his Savior,
Highly values the precious soul of his neighbor,
And pleads and sighs to God for his conversion,
Shows him love, and lures him through his teaching
Away from the earthly; and although he wanders from the right path,
Or does not understand a matter exactly as he does;
Nevertheless, he will exhort him to virtue,
And pave a way to the Heavenly Sion <note>A biblical reference to the "Heavenly Jerusalem," representing the kingdom of God or the ultimate spiritual home of the faithful.</note>.
But one who is blinded by arrogance,
Condemns him whom he finds in error.
O fool! say, how do you dare presume?
God alone is a judge who the conscience
Can fathom, whose judgment never fails:
Therefore, when you see that your brother wanders,
And he will not listen to any exhortations,
Pray to God that he may not be lost through this.
Avoid him: but do not yet condemn him.
John <note>Referring to the Apostle John. An ancient tradition tells of John encountering the heretic Cerinthus in a public bathhouse in Ephesus and fleeing immediately.</note> whom one sees quickly fleeing,
Because he has heard Cerinthus is in the bath,
Does not want to come into conversation with that erring spirit;
Why? He who is convinced of his evil,
Wanders willfully, and does not bend the hard heart,
Remains—to safeguard the church from discord—
No longer among the congregation of God’s holy ones.

> John <note>The Apostle John, traditional author of the Fourth Gospel and Revelation.</note> possessed the gift of discerning spirits;
> He knew Cerinthus’s <note>Cerinthus (c. 100 AD) was an early Gnostic teacher who was considered a heretic by the early Church for his views on the nature of Christ and the creation of the world.</note> heart and his grisly resolution,
> To entice the early Christian church into sin;
> Therefore he shuns him, and flees the Ephesian bathhouse <note>A famous tradition recorded by the early church father Irenaeus; John reportedly fled the building fearing it would collapse because the "enemy of the truth" was inside.</note>:
> But for us, the ignorant, who err in many matters,
> Judgment is not commanded—only caution <term>circumspection</term> <note>original: "omzigtigheid"; the quality of being wary and unwilling to take risks, or being careful to consider all circumstances.</note>.

SCENES OF THE
LXXXIII.

<meta>Ghosting of text from a previous page (notably the heading "Eighty-second Scene") is visible through the paper.</meta>

### Eighty-third Scene.

When superstition has blinded a man,
He condemns everything that conflicts with his own views;
Everything others aim for seems evil to him,
He cherishes those who love his own delusions;
Indeed, one man dares to damn another.
One does not live by the counsel of godliness or reason.
One promotes what was implanted from youth,
And dares to call anyone who does not agree a heretic.
What great persecution has arisen from this,
With Christendom as if stuffed with fables,
Which Rome <note>original: "Romen"; used here to represent the Roman Catholic Church and its authorities.</note> wants to pass off as the Gospel to everyone,
By means of ropes, fire, and swords <note>original: "fabels"; likely an OCR error or archaic spelling for "sabels" (sabers), as it is grouped with "ropes" and "fire" as instruments of execution.</note>.
This was shown in ancient times by Licius <note>likely a reference to a figure from early Christian history or a cautionary tale about religious hair-splitting.</note>,
Who unexpectedly stepped upon a play-ball;
Suddenly it was thought that he had defiled it,
And everyone considered this a terrifying omen.
He is despised here by the children because of this,
Labeled a heretic, and driven from street to street.
The delusions of the elders can leave an impression on a child
That resists truth, virtue, and reason.

LXXXIV.

### *Eighty-fourth Scene.*

Behold this strange occurrence.
Here they stoke the fire, in which the ball,
That Licius has stepped upon,
Must be solemnly purified;
So that the heretical venom <note>original: "ketterſche fenyn"; the author uses the metaphor of poison or "venom" to describe how people of the time viewed the influence of those with differing religious beliefs.</note>
Should not penetrate anyone's limbs.

Blinded and foolish Priesthood,
O asses with a monk’s cowl, <note>original: "Munnikskap"; a hooded garment worn by monks. Here, the author uses it as a symbol of religious office to mock those he considers hypocritical or ignorant.</note>
Who can gape at fables,
And spread a thousand lies,
With which you yourself were first misled,
How much you resemble these boys.

But you who are better enlightened,
And devise such fables,
To strengthen <term>superstition</term> <note>original: "bygeloof"; a belief or practice resulting from ignorance, fear of the unknown, or trust in magic or chance.</note> thereby,
What a judgment you bring upon your own head,
When you mislead the people so falsely,
With your invented miracles! <note>original: "wonderwerken"; here referring to "pious frauds" or fabricated supernatural events used to manipulate the faithful.</note>

You scold and scream of heresy;
So that your stall <note>original: "uw kraam"; literally a market stall. The author implies that the church's teachings are a commercial "business" or trade.</note> may be salable,
And you throw sand in the eyes of the innocent:
But God, who sees your wickedness,
You do not blind His eyes so;
Tremble! Tremble at last before His power!

### Eighty-sixth Scene.

When Athanasius <note>likely referring to a patriarch or leader during the 5th-century Christological disputes. While the author names Athanasius, this specific event—adding "who was crucified for us" to the Trisagion hymn—is historically associated with Peter the Fuller.</note> had caused to be set in a hymn,
Which was sung daily in the Christian temples,
These words, *who once hung upon the cross for us*, <note>original: "die voor ons wel eer aan ’t kruishout hong." This refers to the controversial addition to the "Trisagion" (Thrice Holy) hymn. Critics argued this implied that the Divine nature of the entire Trinity suffered on the cross, a view known as Theopaschism.</note>
This was condemned by the ecclesiastical laws.

Immediately the cry of riot is raised, furious church trumpets are sounded,
And they scourge even the sovereign with a sharp tongue,
As if he had welcomed <term>heresy</term> <note>original: "kettery"; a belief or opinion contrary to established religious doctrine, often punished by the state and church.</note> into the empire,
Which they seek to prevent with bans and lightning-bolts of excommunication.

Constantinople beholds this bloody spectacle:
How the mob, in a rage, dares to strike a monk’s head from his body,
And carry it upon a spear in mockery through all the streets.

Everyone cries out in bitterness (who does not shudder reading this!):
"*This man has been an enemy of the <term>Trinity</term>!*" <note>original: "Drieëenigheid"; the Christian doctrine defining one God in three coequal, coeternal Persons: the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.</note>
O church strife! Foul harpy, what sorrows and plagues you bring forth!

While the previous scene focused on the symbolic "purification" of objects touched by dissenting bishops, this scene moves to Constantinople to depict the lethal consequences of liturgical disputes, specifically regarding the wording of the "Trisagion" hymn. The text documents a specific riot in 512 AD.</meta>

->SCENES OF THE<-

## Eighty-sixth Scene.

When Athanasius <note>Though the author writes "Athanazius," the historical event—adding "who was crucified for us" to the hymn—occurred under the reign of Emperor Anastasius I and was championed by the patriarch Peter the Fuller.</note> had caused to be set in a hymn,
Which was sung daily in the Christian temples,
These words, *who once hung upon the cross for us*, <note>original: "die voor ons wel eer aan 't kruishout hong." This addition to the "Trisagion" (Thrice Holy) hymn was controversial because critics felt it suggested that the entire Trinity—including God the Father—suffered on the cross, a view known as Theopaschism.</note>
This was condemned by the ecclesiastical laws.

Immediately a riot is cried out, the furious church trumpets are sounded, <note>A metaphor for inflammatory sermons and calls to action from the pulpit.</note>
And they scourge even the sovereign with a sharp tongue,
As if he had welcomed <term>heresy</term> <note>original: "kettery"; beliefs that contradict official church doctrine.</note> into the empire,
Which they seek to prevent with bans and lightning-bolts. <note>original: "ban en blixem"; a reference to the "thunderbolts" of excommunication used by the church to cast out members.</note>

Constantinople beholds this bloody spectacle:
How the mob, in a rage, dares to strike a monk’s head from his body,
And carry it upon a spear in mockery through all the streets.

Everyone cries out in bitterness (who does not shudder reading this!):
“*This man has been an enemy of the <term>Trinity</term>!*” <note>The Christian doctrine that God exists as three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note>
O church strife! Vile harpy, <note>A predatory monster from mythology, used here to personify the destructive nature of religious discord.</note> what sorrow and plagues you bring forth!

<meta>This page transitions from the general religious riots in Constantinople described on the previous page to a specific instance of individual martyrdom. SCENES OF THE
LXXXVII.

### Eighty-seventh Scene.

Arnold, who had shown the <term>clergy</term> <note>original: "geeſtlykheid"; the body of religious leaders and officials.</note>
Their own horrors before their eyes,
In hope that someone might be moved
And led onto the path of virtue,
Must suffer innocently in the flames,
As if he were bound by <term>heresy</term>. <note>original: "ketteryën"; the crime of holding beliefs that contradicted established church doctrine, a charge frequently used against reformers.</note>

Hypocrisy has accused him,
As if he would bar the Lord’s Supper
And <term>baptism</term> <note>original: "doopzel"; the sacrament of admission into the Christian church.</note> from the church;
But no, a glorious heavenly light
Kindled his pure heart within,
To win souls through faith.

He was enlightened ray upon ray,
Faith and love gave him hope,
His heart stood open to his Savior,
With Him he held the <term>Supper</term>. <note>original: "avondmaal"; the Eucharist or Holy Communion. The poet suggests that Arnold's true communion was a spiritual one with Christ, regardless of the church's rituals.</note>
Show, Hypocrites, freely your wolf-teeth!
The body you can: but no soul can you burn.

<summary>This page describes the martyrdom of Arnold, a reformer who was burned at the stake after being falsely accused of heresy for criticizing the corruption of the clergy. The poem contrasts the "wolf-like" cruelty of his persecutors with the inner spiritual light and faith of the martyr.</summary>

->*Arnold, Bishop of Brescia, burned as a heretic.*<-

The pious Arnold must, because he punished the evil conduct
Of the Shepherds <note>original: "Herd'ren"; a metaphorical term for the clergy or church leadership.</note>, suffer at a stake;
Now, out of fear of scorn, one lets the reins of church discipline slip

SCENES OF THE

## *Eighty-eighth Scene.*

Arnold <note>original: "Arnulfus"; the Latinized name for Arnold of Brescia, a 12th-century reformer.</note>, by whose golden mouth <note>A reference to his legendary eloquence; "Golden Mouth" was a title traditionally given to the most gifted preachers of the early church.</note>
So many were converted;
Who from the Old and New Covenant <note>The Old and New Testaments of the Bible.</note>
Had taught everyone the will of God;
Who even in Rome was esteemed
Like a great Prophet,
Is brought into the prison-house,
And overwhelmed with sorrow.
God’s Angel (it is said) had descended
From heaven's clear vault,
Radiant with the light of the Most High,
And came for the man’s comfort,
Who, in a dreary wilderness <note>original: "naare Woesteny"; this likely refers to Arnold's periods of exile or his spiritual isolation while opposing the church hierarchy.</note>
Cried out to God in prayer;
So that the church might be saved
From the dominion of hypocrites;
Whereupon this Angel commands him,
That he, in the Vatican,
To him who soothes himself with luxury <note>A pointed critique of the Pope and the high-ranking clergy living in opulence.</note>,
Should make God’s word understood,
And boldly oppose pride and greed;
So that God’s love would be spread
Through unadulterated doctrine <note>Teaching that is "pure" or "unpolluted" by human tradition or corruption.</note>:
But Rome (what an atrocity!)
Strangles the Christian hero <note>History records that Arnold was hanged (or strangled) before his body was burned and his ashes thrown into the Tiber River to prevent his followers from claiming his remains as relics.</note>.
How little does he know his own happiness
Who chooses the earthly over the heavenly!

<summary>This page describes the spiritual mission of Arnold of Brescia, depicting him as a prophet-like figure commanded by an angel to confront the greed of the Vatican, ending in his execution by the authorities in Rome.</summary>

In vain, Arnold <note>original: "Arnulfus"; refering to the 12th-century religious reformer Arnold of Brescia.</note>, did you perform your duty:
Obstinacy in evil is deaf to reason:
Your Christian zeal led to your being strangled in chains.
Alas! Would that the clergy—who have escaped blind superstition
Through the unsullied sun of truth—

SCENES OF THE
LXXXIX.

### Eighty-ninth Scene.

THE Shepherd who faithfully guarded God's lambs,
Is torn apart in the arena <note>The Dutch word "perk" refers to an enclosed space or ring, here specifically the Roman amphitheater.</note> by hungry beasts;
<margin>◦</margin> His soul, which glows in a fire of heavenly love,
Hears not how the savage heathens roar around the ring.

Let Antioch celebrate the man's passing with joy,
While God's lambs feel this in their tender hearts;
His writings shall long adorn the true church,
And he receives the fate for which he long has yearned. <note>Ignatius famously wrote in his Letter to the Romans: "I am the wheat of God, and let me be ground by the teeth of the wild beasts, that I may be found the pure bread of Christ."</note>

Let the lion of the abyss <note>A reference to Satan or the personification of death.</note> roar and whet his sharp claws;
He can neither conquer nor distress the Christian hero;
The Lion of Judah <note>A title for Jesus Christ, referencing his victory over death (Revelation 5:5).</note> stands for him in the arena.

Thus a pure soul cannot succumb in the struggle;
So shall Ignatius pluck the palms of heaven,
And rest eternally after his apostolic work.

<term>Apoſtoliſch werk</term> (Apostolic work): This refers to the labors of the "Apostolic Fathers," the generation of church leaders who personally knew the original Apostles or were directly influenced by them. Ignatius was a disciple of John the Evangelist.

<summary>This page commemorates the martyrdom of Ignatius, the Bishop of Antioch, who was sentenced to death by wild beasts in Rome. The poem celebrates his eagerness to face death for his faith and the lasting impact of the letters he wrote to early Christian communities.</summary>

<keywords>Ignatius of Antioch, martyrdom, Roman arena, Lion of Judah, early Church Fathers, apostolic work, Jan Luyken</keywords>

SCENES OF THE... <note>Likely "Scenes of the Martyrs," a common title for works by Jan Luyken.</note>

### Ninetieth Scene.

See Polycarp, with a soul at peace,
Praising the King of the universe for His grace;
The flame surrounds the man's body, like a fiery oven,
His body shines like gold, and glitters in the glow.

A pleasant scent, which refreshes and feeds the heart,
Like a scent of roses in Sharon's noble gardens, <note>Sharon is a fertile coastal plain in Israel, often used in literature to symbolize beauty and abundance.</note>
Spreads among the people, which lifts the heart upward,
Converted by this sight, despite the courtly rabble. <note>original: "hofgebroed," a derogatory term for the corrupt officials or the antagonistic crowd.</note>

What scent can compare to the scent of such a funeral pyre,
Kindled by the power of Divine rays,
Even if Arabia were stripped of its frankincense forests?

The woodpile on which you burned Caesar's corpse,
Which cost fortunes, is nothing compared to this, oh Tyrants!
How glorious would his fate have been, had he been thus deified. <note>This refers to the Roman practice of "apotheosis," where an Emperor was declared a god after death. The poet argues that Polycarp’s Christian martyrdom is more truly "divine" than a Roman Emperor's state funeral.</note>

# FIRST CHRISTIANS.
## XC.
**90**

[Image Description: A detailed engraving by Jan Luyken depicting the martyrdom of Saint Polycarp. Polycarp is shown seated calmly atop a large funeral pyre. The flames do not touch him but instead swell out in the shape of an arch or a sail, illuminated with light. A large crowd of spectators and Roman soldiers surrounds the scene, some with arms raised in prayer or astonishment. ->Polycarp burned.<-

Oh Roman Emperor! Can the brilliance of your throne compare
To Polycarp’s throne, so full of glory? Oh, no:
He shines within an arch of flickering rays
Of a golden flame, while through his prayers
And thankful echoes of sacrifice, he lifts his soul on high.
Thus does God’s mighty hand know how to dampen the fire’s glow. <note>According to the historical account *The Martyrdom of Polycarp*, the fire did not burn his body but formed an "appearance of a baked loaf" or an arch like a ship's sail filled by the wind, surrounding him with a sweet aroma.</note>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of early Christian figures and their trials. While the previous page focused on the martyrdom of Polycarp, the narrative now shifts to the 4th century to recount the exile of John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), the Archbishop of Constantinople known for his powerful oratory and his clashes with the imperial court.</meta>

SCENES OF THE [FIRST CHRISTIANS]
91.

### *Ninety-first Scene.*

Chrysostom is driven away to Armenia,
Because he sincerely preaches the word of the Lord,
Through the pure power of the spirit, he breaks the hardened heart,
And refuses to flatter the one elevated to the imperial throne.

<margin>* Chrysostom means "golden-mouth."</margin>
No proud Empress <note>Refers to Aelia Eudoxia, the wife of Emperor Arcadius. She took John’s sermons against the vanity and luxury of the ruling class as personal insults.</note> can make this hero tremble,
Whose * golden mouth speaks out against the wickedness of the great,
Which ignites the corrupt court into a frenzy,
And even makes the clergy <note>John’s strict reforms and demands for clerical poverty made him many enemies among the high-ranking priests and bishops of Constantinople.</note> plot against his life.

But he, who never stepped a foot away from Heaven's path of salvation,
Considers their persecution as a precious treasure,
Comforted to wander here on earth in exile.

He knows that Christ had no fixed dwelling place here,
And sees that the narrow way leads to the city of peace,
In which his Savior shall receive him according to his worth.

<term>Chrysostom</term>: From the Greek *Chrysostomos*, meaning "golden-mouthed," a title given to John of Antioch for his legendary eloquence. <term>Armenia</term>: The remote region on the fringes of the Byzantine Empire where John was banished to silence his influence. <term>Golden-mouth</term>: A literal translation of his epithet, signifying that his words were as precious and pure as gold. <term>Exile</term>: Banishment from one's home; John died in 407 AD during a forced march in the heat and rain to an even more remote location. <term>City of peace</term>: A poetic reference to the New Jerusalem or Heaven. <term>Sincere</term>: Meaning "unfeigned" or without hypocrisy, reflecting John's refusal to soften his moral message for the sake of politics. <term>Clergy</term>: The body of all people ordained for religious duties; here, specifically the ambitious bishops who participated in the "Synod of the Oak" to depose John.

<summary>This page illustrates the exile of Saint John Chrysostom, highlighting his refusal to flatter the Byzantine Empress and his steadfastness in the face of political and clerical persecution. It frames his banishment to Armenia as a spiritual journey following the path of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>John Chrysostom, Empress Eudoxia, Armenia, exile, golden-mouth, Constantinople, religious integrity, Byzantine Empire</keywords>

# EARLY CHRISTIANS.
# XCI.

### THE NINETY-FIRST SCENE

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the exile of John Chrysostom. He is portrayed as an elderly, bearded man in long robes, holding a book, being escorted by several Roman soldiers in helmets and armor carrying spears. They are walking through a rugged, mountainous landscape. In the bottom right corner of the plate, the artist's name "T. Luyken." and the number "91" are etched.</image-desc>

->*Chrysostom led into exile.*<-

Byzantium’s Golden-mouth <note>original: "Guldemond"; a literal translation of the Greek "Chrysostomos," referring to John's legendary skill as a preacher.</note>, must you wander as an exile,
Just like your namesake <note>Referring to John the Apostle (the Evangelist), who was also sent into exile on the island of Patmos.</note>, that most beloved messenger of the Cross,
Take heart, for you shall never lack the help of your Creator:
It is thus that one strives through the cross toward the eternal Fatherland.
In this wilderness, let your piety never waver,
Though you find here no Patmos filled with heavenly visions. <note>While the Apostle John received the Book of Revelation during his exile on Patmos, Chrysostom’s exile in the harsh mountains of Armenia was marked by physical suffering and isolation without such recorded mystical visions.</note>

<meta>This page follows the account of John Chrysostom’s exile described on the previous page. SCENES OF THE
XCII.

### The Ninety-Second Scene.

Here Chrysostom’s bones are laid to rest
By the good congregation,
In Constantine’s renowned city <note>original: "Konstantyns vermaarde Stadt"; referring to Constantinople, where Chrysostom had served as Archbishop.</note>,
Which saw him sent away in misery as an exile
Thirty years ago,
And now regards these remains <note>original: "rif"; literally meaning a carcass or skeleton, here used for the saint's holy relics.</note> as a treasure.

Death, a passage for the godly
To come before the throne of God,
Purified of earthly dross;
Death, a terror for all the wicked
Who disregard the favor of Heaven,
Mows everything down with its scythe.

Death regards neither the villain nor the saint,
Nothing is safe from its arrows;
Let each take care that he is not caught by surprise.
Happy are those people to be considered
Who dare to await it boldly,
Like a welcome guest.

Let one reflect that we die daily,
So long as we lack the true life;
Since this life, like a dream,
Shall vanish in a single moment,
And each must appear before the judgment of God,
Whether with joy, terror, or awe.

<term>Chrysostom</term> (John Chrysostom, a prominent early Church Father known for his powerful preaching), <term>Constantine’s City</term> (Constantinople), <term>Death</term>, <term>God’s Judgment</term>, <term>Exile</term>, <term>Remains</term>, <term>Congregation</term>

<summary>This page describes the return of John Chrysostom's remains to Constantinople thirty years after his exile, using the event to meditate on the impartiality of death and the necessity of preparing for the afterlife.</summary>

While the previous scene focused on his suffering and departure into the wilderness, this page describes the return of his remains to the city thirty years later, shifting from historical narrative to a meditation on mortality.</meta>

SCENES OF THE

## *The Ninety-second Scene.*

Here the bones of Chrysostom
Are buried by the good congregation,
In the renowned City of Constantine <note>Constantinople, the capital of the Eastern Roman Empire.</note>,
Which saw him sent away as an exile in misery
Thirty years ago,
And now prizes these remains <note>original: "rif"; literally meaning a carcass or skeleton, here referring to his holy relics.</note> as a treasure.

Death, a passage for the pious
To come before the throne of God,
Purified of earthly dross;
Death, a terror for all the wicked
Who neglect the favor of Heaven,
Mows everything down with its scythe.

Death regards neither villain nor saint,
Nothing is safe from its arrows;
Let each see to it that he is not caught by surprise.
Happy are those people to be considered
Who dare to wait for it boldly,
Like a welcomed guest.

Let us think that we die daily,
As long as we lack the true life <note>In this context, the "true life" refers to eternal life in heaven, compared to which earthly life is merely a shadow or dream.</note>;
Since this life, like a dream,
Shall vanish in an instant,
And everyone will appear before the judgment of God,
Whether with joy, terror, or awe.

<page-num>XCII.</page-num>

**Vocabulary:** Chrysostom, Constantine, death, the pious, judgment of God, exile

<summary>This page describes the translation of John Chrysostom's relics back to Constantinople in 438 AD and uses the event to reflect on the inevitability of death for both the righteous and the wicked.</summary>
<keywords>John Chrysostom, Constantinople, relics, memento mori, death, divine judgment, exile</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page concludes the narrative of John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), the Archbishop of Constantinople. While previous pages detailed his exile and death in the harsh conditions of Comana, this page depicts the "Translation of the Relics"—the ceremonial return of his remains to the capital city in 438 AD, thirty years after his passing.</meta>

FIRST CHRISTIANS.
XCII.

->Chrysostom’s bones buried in Constantinople.<-

Constantinople drove you out during your life,
   Chrysostom; but now, now she opens her lap <note>original: "schoot"; figuratively referring to the city's embrace or the earth of the homeland.</note>,
To finally give rest to the thirty-year-old bones,
   Which she once begrudged you: Her hatred died with your death.
But this passion <note>original: "hertstocht"; here referring to the intense, fickle emotions of the city's populace and leaders.</note> grew so powerful in its strength,
That she once dared to despise the pious even after their mortal fate <note>original: "sterflot"; refers to the destiny of death or the state of being deceased.</note>.

->T H E   E N D.<-

<term>Chrysostom</term> (John Chrysostom, known as "Golden-Mouthed" for his eloquence, a Church Father who was exiled due to his critiques of the imperial court), <term>Constantinople</term> (the capital of the Eastern Roman Empire, modern-day Istanbul), <term>Bones</term> (the holy relics of the saint, which were returned to the Church of the Holy Apostles), <term>Pious</term> (the "vroomen," referring to the godly or devout followers of Christ), <term>Jan Luyken</term> (the noted Dutch poet and engraver who created this work).

<summary>This final scene depicts the solemn return and burial of John Chrysostom’s remains in Constantinople, thirty years after his exile. The accompanying poem reflects on how the city’s former persecution and hatred were transformed into veneration after the saint's death.</summary>

<keywords>John Chrysostom, Constantinople, relics, burial, repentance, Church Fathers, Jan Luyken, exile, redemption</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page concludes the narrative of John Chrysostom. Following the previous page's meditation on his exile and the nature of death, this final entry depicts the "Translation of the Relics"—the ceremonial return of his remains to Constantinople in 438 AD, thirty years after his death in exile, marking his posthumous vindication.</meta>

FIRST CHRISTIANS.
XCII.

<image-desc>An etching by Jan Luyken depicting the translation of the remains of Saint John Chrysostom. Several figures are gathered around a rectangular wooden box containing a skull and other bones. One man is kneeling in prayer or reverence on the right, while others assist in lowering or handling the casket near a freshly dug grave. In the left foreground, a shovel and a pickaxe lie on the ground. The background shows a detailed cityscape of Constantinople, prominently featuring the dome and structure of the Hagia Sophia. The image is signed 'J. Luiken' in the lower right corner of the plate.</image-desc>

->The bones of Chrysostom buried in Constantinople.<-

> Constantinople drove you out during your lifetime,
> Chrysostom; but now, now she opens her bosom,
> To finally give rest to these thirty-year-old bones,
> Which she once begrudged you: her hatred died with your death.
> But this devotion <note>original: "hertstocht"; literally "passion," here referring to the religious fervor and change of heart among the people.</note> gradually grew so strong in power,
> That she [the city] once dared to despise the pious <note>original: "vroomen"; the godly or devout followers.</note> even after their mortal fate.

-> T H E   E N D . <-

<vocab>Chrysostom, Constantinople, bones (remains), buried, Jan Luyken, the pious</vocab>

<summary>This final page describes the return of John Chrysostom's remains to Constantinople thirty years after his death, highlighting the city's transition from persecuting the living saint to venerating his relics.</summary>
